# Chapter 4. Dreamers, Authority, and the Archangel's Restraint (Jude 8–10)

## A. The Text

> **8** In like manner, nevertheless, those dreaming indeed defile the flesh, and they disregard lordship, and they speak evil of glorious [ones], **9** yet Michael, the chief messenger, when disputing with the Devil, reasoning about the body of Moses, did not dare to bring against [him] a judgment of slander, but said, “The LORD rebuke you!” **10** But these, indeed, whatever [things] they have not known, they speak evil of; and whatever [they know], like the irrational beasts, they understand naturally—in these they are corrupted;
>
> — Jude 8–10, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

Having set out three examples of judgment, Jude now applies them. The intruders behave "in like manner." Relying on their dreams, they do three things: they defile the flesh, they reject authority, and they slander heavenly beings.

To expose the arrogance of the third charge, Jude tells a story his readers evidently knew. When Michael, the greatest of the archangels, disputed with the Devil over Moses' body, he did not presume to pronounce judgment on his adversary on his own authority. He left that to God: "The LORD rebuke you!"

The intruders show no such restraint. They mock what they do not understand. Meanwhile, the things they do understand, their instincts and appetites, are understood the way animals understand them, and those very things are destroying them.

The passage turns on a contrast between **presumption** and **reverence**. The intruders claim special insight through dreams, yet they are ignorant of spiritual realities and governed by animal instinct. Michael, who stands in the presence of God, knows the limits of his authority.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "In like manner, nevertheless"

The opening Ὁμοίως μέντοι καὶ οὗτοι (*Homoiōs mentoi kai houtoi*), literally "In the same way, however, these also," ties the intruders to the examples of vv. 5–7. The particle μέντοι (*mentoi*), "nevertheless, yet," adds a note of surprise or protest. *In spite of* these well-known examples of judgment, the intruders do the same things. This is the first of the letter's "these" applications.

### "those dreaming"

The participle ἐνυπνιαζόμενοι (*enypniazomenoi*), "dreaming," stands as the description of the intruders that governs all three of the following verbs. The LSV's "those dreaming indeed defile the flesh" preserves this: the dreaming is the source of the behavior.

What does Jude mean? Some interpreters take it figuratively, as if the intruders lived in a dream world of self-delusion or fantasized about immorality. The background of the word points elsewhere. In the Greek Old Testament, the same verb describes the false prophet of Deuteronomy 13: "When a prophet or a dreamer of a dream arises in your midst … saying, Let us go after other gods … you do not listen to the words of that prophet or to that dreamer of the dream" (Deut 13:1–3, LSV). The Septuagint uses ἐνυπνιαζόμενος, Jude's word, for that "dreamer of a dream."[^c4-1] Jeremiah denounces prophets "who prophesy falsehood in My Name, saying, I have dreamed, I have dreamed" (Jer 23:25, LSV), and God says, "I [am] against the prophets of false dreams" (Jer 23:32, LSV).

Dreams were not suspect in themselves. Joel promised that in the last days "Your old men dream dreams" (Joel 2:28, LSV), and Peter announced the fulfillment of that promise at Pentecost (Acts 2:17). The problem was dreams claimed as divine revelation that led people away from God's revealed will. Jude's intruders seem to have claimed visions or revelations that authorized their conduct. That would explain why Jude stresses that the faith was delivered "once" (v. 3). No new revelation can overturn it.

### "indeed defile the flesh"

The first of three charges, σάρκα μὲν μιαίνουσιν (*sarka men miainousin*), uses the verb μιαίνω (*miainō*), "to stain, defile, soil," in a moral sense.[^c4-2] "The flesh" here means the body. The charge corresponds to Sodom's sexual immorality (v. 7) and to the intruders' turning grace into "licentiousness" (v. 4).

The three charges are linked by the Greek particles μέν … δέ … δέ (*men … de … de*), "on the one hand … on the other … and." The LSV's "indeed … and … and" reflects this. The construction holds the three together as aspects of a single pattern of life.

### "and they disregard lordship"

The second charge, κυριότητα δὲ ἀθετοῦσιν (*kyriotēta de athetousin*), uses ἀθετέω (*atheteō*), "to set aside, reject, nullify,"[^c4-3] with the object κυριότης (*kyriotēs*), "lordship, dominion." What lordship do they reject?

1. **The lordship of the Lord.** The word is derived from κύριος (*kyrios*, "lord"), and Jude has just said that the intruders deny "our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ" (v. 4). Rejecting "lordship" would then mean rejecting the authority of Christ. Clement of Alexandria read it this way: "despise dominion … that is, the only Lord, who is truly our Lord, Jesus Christ."[^c4-4]
2. **Angelic powers.** In Eph 1:21 and Col 1:16, "lordship" (κυριότης) is one of a list of angelic ranks: "principality, and authority, and might, and lordship" (Eph 1:21, LSV). Since the next charge concerns "glorious [ones]," Jude might mean angelic authorities here too.
3. **Human church leaders.** Some have suggested that the intruders despised the leadership of the community.

The first interpretation fits the immediate context best, since it echoes v. 4. It also fits the contrast with the angels of v. 6, who left their place under God's authority. The singular "lordship," without an article, may also suggest an abstract principle: the intruders reject the very idea of being under authority.

### "and they speak evil of glorious [ones]"

The third charge is the most puzzling. The verb βλασφημέω (*blasphēmeō*) means "to slander, revile, speak abusively of." The object δόξας (*doxas*), literally "glories," refers to glorious beings, almost certainly angels. The LSV's bracketed "[ones]" signals that the Greek has only the plural noun. Second Peter's parallel confirms the angelic reference: "they are not afraid to speak evil of glorious ones, whereas messengers, being greater in strength and power, do not bear a slanderous judgment against them before the LORD" (2 Pet 2:10–11, LSV).

Why would anyone slander angels? Jude does not say, and scholars have proposed several explanations:

- **Disparaging the angels as guardians of the moral law.** Jewish and early Christian tradition held that the Law of Moses was given "through messengers" (Gal 3:19; Acts 7:53; Heb 2:2). If the intruders taught that grace freed them from the moral law, they might have belittled the angels associated with that law. This explanation has been influential in modern scholarship.[^c4-5]
- **Mocking evil powers.** If the "glorious ones" include fallen angels (some argue this from the Michael example, where the slander concerns the Devil), the intruders may have treated spiritual evil with contempt, claiming that their spiritual status put them beyond its reach.
- **General irreverence** toward the whole unseen order of God's creation.

The example that follows, Michael's restraint toward the Devil, suggests that Jude's main point is the intruders' presumption. Whatever beings they slandered, they spoke with an authority they did not possess.

### "yet Michael, the chief messenger"

Michael is one of only two angels named in the Protestant canon of Scripture; the other is Gabriel. In Daniel he is "Michael, first of the chief heads" and "the great head, who is standing up for the sons of your people" (Dan 10:13; 12:1, LSV), the guardian of Israel. In Revelation, "Michael and his messengers warred against the dragon" (Rev 12:7, LSV). Jude calls him ὁ ἀρχάγγελος (*ho archangelos*), "the archangel," literally "the chief messenger," as the LSV renders it. The only other New Testament use of the word is in 1 Thess 4:16, "with the voice of a chief-messenger" (LSV).

The choice of Michael heightens the contrast. If anyone had the standing to condemn the Devil, it was the commander of heaven's armies.

### "when disputing with the Devil, reasoning about the body of Moses"

Two legal or debating verbs describe the encounter. διακρινόμενος (*diakrinomenos*) means "contending, disputing," often in a judicial sense. διελέγετο (*dielegeto*) means "was arguing, debating." The scene is a dispute, perhaps a kind of court case, "about the body of Moses" (περὶ τοῦ Μωϋσέως σώματος, *peri tou Mōyseōs sōmatos*), over who had rights to it.

The Old Testament says only this about Moses' death: "Moses, servant of the Lord, dies there in the land of Moab, according to the command of YHWH; and He buries him in a valley in the land of Moab, opposite Beth-Peor, and no man has known his burying place to this day" (Deut 34:5–6, LSV). The mysterious burial, done by God himself in an unknown place, invited speculation, and a tradition grew up that angels were involved. Jude's version, the only surviving ancient form of the story that names a dispute between Michael and the Devil, is explored in the deep dive below.

### "did not dare to bring against [him] a judgment of slander"

The key phrase οὐκ ἐτόλμησεν κρίσιν ἐπενεγκεῖν βλασφημίας (*ouk etolmēsen krisin epenenkein blasphēmias*) can be read in two ways.

1. **"He did not dare to pronounce a slanderous (or abusive) judgment against him."** The genitive βλασφημίας (*blasphēmias*) describes the kind of judgment: an insulting, reviling condemnation. Michael did not abuse the Devil, even though the Devil deserved condemnation. He left judgment to God. This is the traditional reading (KJV: "durst not bring against him a railing accusation") and the one most translations follow.
2. **"He did not presume to condemn him for slander."** The genitive names the charge: Michael did not pronounce a verdict of "slander" against the Devil. On this reading, which draws on a reconstruction of the lost ending of the *Testament of Moses* (see the deep dive), the Devil had slandered Moses, perhaps by accusing him of murder. Michael, though in the right, did not presume to judge the Devil for this slander and appealed to God instead.[^c4-6]

Both readings reach the same point. Michael, though an archangel and in the right, recognized that judgment over a fellow spiritual being belonged to God alone. The intruders, mere humans, pass judgment freely on beings far above them.

The verb ἐτόλμησεν (*etolmēsen*), "he dared," is important. Jude does not say Michael was unable to condemn the Devil, or that the Devil deserved no condemnation. He says Michael *did not dare*. The restraint was reverence, not weakness.

### "but said, 'The LORD rebuke you!'"

Michael's words, Ἐπιτιμήσαι σοι κύριος (*Epitimēsai soi kyrios*), use the **optative** of wish: "May the Lord rebuke you!" This is the second optative in Jude, after "be multiplied" in v. 2. The LSV prints "LORD" in capitals, indicating that the reference is to the God of Israel.

The words echo Zechariah's vision of Joshua the high priest, accused by Satan before the angel of the LORD: "And YHWH says to Satan: 'YHWH pushes against you, O Satan, / Indeed, YHWH pushes against you'" (Zech 3:2, LSV). The Greek Septuagint renders the same words as Ἐπιτιμήσαι Κύριος ἐν σοί, διάβολε, "May the Lord rebuke you, O Devil," nearly identical to Jude.[^c4-7] In both scenes, a heavenly figure facing the accuser leaves the rebuke to God.

Zechariah 3 will return in Jude 23: the high priest Joshua is "a brand delivered from fire" and is "clothed with filthy garments" (Zech 3:2–3, LSV), images that reappear in Jude's call to snatch others "out of fire" and to hate the "coat having been stained from the flesh."

### "But these, indeed, whatever [things] they have not known, they speak evil of"

Verse 10 returns to the intruders, "these" (οὗτοι, *houtoi*), with a pointed contrast built on two verbs of knowing:

- ὅσα μὲν οὐκ οἴδασιν βλασφημοῦσιν (*hosa men ouk oidasin blasphēmousin*), "whatever they do not know, they slander." The verb οἶδα (*oida*) here refers to knowledge of spiritual realities, the unseen world of angels and authority. Those who claimed revelatory dreams are, in Jude's judgment, ignorant of the very realm they speak about.

### "and whatever [they know], like the irrational beasts, they understand naturally—in these they are corrupted"

- ὅσα δὲ φυσικῶς ὡς τὰ ἄλογα ζῷα ἐπίστανται (*hosa de physikōs hōs ta aloga zōa epistantai*), "whatever they understand by instinct, like unreasoning animals." The second verb, ἐπίσταμαι (*epistamai*), means "to understand, know how." The adverb φυσικῶς (*physikōs*), "naturally, by nature,"[^c4-8] found only here in the New Testament, describes knowledge that comes from instinct, not reason or revelation. The phrase τὰ ἄλογα ζῷα (*ta aloga zōa*), "the irrational animals," literally "the creatures without *logos*," without reason or speech, was a stock expression for brute beasts.

Then the verdict: ἐν τούτοις φθείρονται (*en toutois phtheirontai*), "by these things they are destroyed" or "in these they are corrupted." The verb φθείρω (*phtheirō*) means both "to corrupt" and "to destroy, ruin," and Jude may intend both: the appetites they follow corrupt them morally and are leading them to destruction.

The irony is sharp. The intruders claim higher knowledge through dreams. In reality they are ignorant of spiritual things, and the only knowledge they truly possess is the instinctive knowledge of appetite, which they share with animals and which is ruining them. Clement of Alexandria paraphrased: "He means that they eat, and drink, and indulge in uncleanness, and says that they do other things that are common to them with animals, devoid of reason."[^c4-9] Paul's description of those whose "god [is] the belly, and whose glory [is] in their shame" (Phil 3:19, LSV) is similar.

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ὁμοίως | *homoiōs* | likewise, in the same way | "In like manner" | Links to vv. 5–7. |
| μέντοι | *mentoi* | nevertheless, yet | "nevertheless" | Adversative: despite the examples. |
| ἐνυπνιάζομαι | *enypniazomai* | to dream | "those dreaming" | LXX Deut 13:2–6 (Eng. 13:1–5): the false prophet as "dreamer." |
| μιαίνω | *miainō* | stain, defile | "defile" | Moral defilement. |
| ἀθετέω | *atheteō* | set aside, reject | "disregard" | To nullify what is established. |
| κυριότης | *kyriotēs* | lordship, dominion | "lordship" | Christ's lordship, or angelic powers (Eph 1:21; Col 1:16). |
| δόξαι | *doxai* | glories; glorious beings | "glorious [ones]" | Angels (cf. 2 Pet 2:10–11). |
| βλασφημέω / βλασφημία | *blasphēmeō* / *blasphēmia* | slander, revile / slander | "speak evil of" / "slander" | Not only against God; any abusive speech. |
| ἀρχάγγελος | *archangelos* | archangel, chief messenger | "the chief messenger" | NT: only here and 1 Thess 4:16. |
| διάβολος | *diabolos* | slanderer, accuser; the Devil | "the Devil" | The word itself means "slanderer," fitting the theme of slander. |
| διακρίνομαι | *diakrinomai* | dispute, contend; doubt, waver | "disputing" | Same verb as v. 22 ("doubting"). |
| διαλέγομαι | *dialegomai* | argue, discuss | "reasoning" | |
| τολμάω | *tolmaō* | dare, presume | "did not dare" | Restraint as reverence. |
| κρίσις | *krisis* | judgment, verdict | "a judgment" | |
| ἐπιτιμάω | *epitimaō* | rebuke, censure | "rebuke" | Optative of wish; = LXX Zech 3:2. |
| οἶδα / ἐπίσταμαι | *oida* / *epistamai* | know / understand | "known" / "understand" | Two kinds of knowing. |
| φυσικῶς | *physikōs* | naturally, by instinct | "naturally" | NT hapax. |
| ἄλογος | *alogos* | without reason | "irrational" | "Creatures without reason." |
| φθείρω | *phtheirō* | corrupt; destroy | "corrupted" | Both senses likely. |

**A note on διάβολος.** The Greek word for "Devil" means "slanderer" or "accuser." There is a quiet irony in the scene. Michael refuses to slander the Slanderer, while the intruders slander even "glorious ones." The term *diabolos* is used in the Septuagint of Zech 3:1–2 for the accuser (Hebrew *haśśāṭān*, "the adversary"), which strengthens the connection between Jude 9 and Zechariah 3.

**A note on διακρίνομαι.** The verb describing Michael's dispute will reappear in v. 22 for those who are "doubting" or "wavering." It is a good example of a word whose sense depends entirely on context: "dispute" in v. 9, "doubt" or "waver" (or perhaps "dispute" again) in v. 22. Chapter 12 explores the possibility that Jude intends a link.

### Deep dive: Michael the archangel and the body of Moses

Jude's reference to a dispute over Moses' body is one of the most intriguing puzzles in the New Testament. The Old Testament contains no such story. Where did it come from?

**What the Bible says.** Deuteronomy 34:5–6 records that Moses died "according to the command of YHWH" (literally "at the mouth of YHWH") and that "He buries him … and no man has known his burying place to this day" (LSV). God himself buried Moses, and the grave was unknown. That combination of divine burial and hidden grave naturally invited questions. Who carried out the burial? Why was the grave hidden? Was there opposition?

**What early Christians said about Jude's source.** Several early Christian writers connected Jude 9 to a specific Jewish book:

- **Clement of Alexandria** (c. 200), commenting on Jude 9, writes: "Here he confirms the assumption of Moses."[^c4-10] The Latin translation preserves the phrase *assumptionem Moysi*, which may refer either to a book (the *Assumption of Moses*) or to the event of Moses' being "taken up."
- **Origen** (c. 230), in *On First Principles*, refers to "the work entitled The Ascension of Moses (a little treatise, of which the Apostle Jude makes mention in his Epistle)," in which "the archangel Michael, when disputing with the devil regarding the body of Moses, says that the serpent, being inspired by the devil, was the cause of Adam and Eve's transgression."[^c4-11]
- Later writers, including Didymus the Blind and Gelasius of Cyzicus, also refer to this work. Gelasius quotes from it in a way that later enabled scholars to identify a surviving manuscript.[^c4-12]

**What survives.** In the mid-nineteenth century Antonio Ceriani discovered in the Ambrosian Library in Milan a single, incomplete sixth-century Latin manuscript of a Jewish work in which Moses, before his death, prophesies Israel's history to Joshua. Ceriani published it in 1861 and identified it, through its agreement with a quotation in Gelasius, as the *Assumption of Moses*. R. H. Charles later argued that the surviving text is better called the *Testament of Moses*, since it contains Moses' farewell speech but no account of his being taken up, and that the testament and the assumption may originally have been distinct works or parts of a combined one. The manuscript breaks off before Moses' death. The dispute between Michael and the Devil does not appear in what survives, and it may have stood in the lost ending.[^c4-13]

**Reconstructing the story.** Combining the patristic references, scholars have proposed outlines of the lost scene. In one widely discussed reconstruction, associated especially with Richard Bauckham, Michael is sent to bury Moses. The Devil opposes him and claims the body, either as lord of the material world or by accusing Moses of murder, since Moses had killed an Egyptian (Exod 2:11–12). Michael refuses to pronounce judgment on the Devil's slander and says, "The Lord rebuke you."[^c4-14] Some versions add that the Devil then fled and Michael buried Moses. Because the ending is lost, every reconstruction is provisional. The details above are scholarly proposals, not recovered text.

**How did Jude use the story?** Jude uses it as he uses the examples of vv. 5–7: as a known story with a clear moral point. He assumes his readers know it, mentions only what he needs, and draws a single lesson. He does not quote a book by name or introduce the story as Scripture.

**Did Jude believe the event happened?** Interpreters answer this differently.

- Some hold that Jude, writing under the guidance of the Spirit, affirmed a true event preserved in Jewish tradition. Calvin, for example, judged that since "the Jews at that time had many things from the traditions of the fathers," it was not unreasonable to say "that Jude referred to what had already been handed down for many ages."[^c4-15]
- Others hold that Jude used a familiar story as an illustration, much as a modern preacher might cite a well-known parable or a classic novel, without endorsing its historicity. On this view the authority lies in the moral point, not in the narrative details.
- Still others think the question cannot be settled and is not essential to understanding Jude's argument.

All agree on the point Jude draws: *if the archangel Michael did not presume to pronounce judgment on the Devil, how much less should mere humans slander heavenly beings?* Readers may hold different views about the story's origin and still receive its lesson.

**Why did the story matter in Jude's world?** A story about angels disputing over Moses' body would have resonated with Jewish readers for whom Moses was the supreme prophet and lawgiver, and for whom the hidden grave was a sign of his unique relationship with God. In a community debating authority, revelation, and the moral law, a story in which even the great archangel deferred to God's authority over the body of the lawgiver was a pointed choice.

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Angels and the law.** Jewish tradition held that angels were present at Sinai and mediated the giving of the Law (Deut 33:2 LXX; Jubilees 1:27–29; Acts 7:53; Gal 3:19; Heb 2:2). Stephen speaks of Israel as those "who received the Law by arrangement of messengers" (Acts 7:53, LSV). If the intruders rejected the moral authority of the law of God, it would be natural for them to speak disparagingly of the angels associated with it.

**Dreams and revelation in the ancient world.** Throughout the ancient Mediterranean, dreams were widely regarded as a channel of divine communication. Jewish tradition accepted that God could speak through dreams, as with Joseph and Daniel, but also warned against dreamers who led people astray (Deut 13; Jer 23). Early Christianity valued prophecy and visions while insisting on testing them (1 Cor 14:29; 1 Thess 5:19–21; 1 John 4:1). The *Didache* warned that a prophet's claims were to be judged by his "ways," his conduct.[^c4-16] Jude's "dreamers" fall under this kind of judgment.

**Testaments and "assumptions."** Jewish literature of the period included many "testaments," farewell discourses of patriarchs before death (the *Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs*, the *Testament of Moses*), and stories of figures "taken up" to heaven (Enoch, Elijah, and in some traditions Moses). Jude's references to Moses' body and to Enoch (vv. 14–15) show that he and his readers were at home in this literature.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**Deuteronomy 13:1–5; Jeremiah 23:25–32 (background for "dreaming").** The "dreamer of a dream" who leads people to "go after other gods" (Deut 13:1–2, LSV) is the clearest Old Testament background for Jude's term. The verbal link is strongest in the Greek Old Testament, where Jude's word appears. This is probably a deliberate echo, though Jude does not quote the passage.

**Zechariah 3:1–4 (direct allusion).** Michael's words in Jude 9 match the Septuagint of Zech 3:2 almost exactly. Since Zechariah 3 also supplies the imagery of Jude 23, Jude, or his source, seems to have known the whole scene well.

**2 Peter 2:10–12 (literary parallel).** Second Peter generalizes Jude's example: "messengers, being greater in strength and power, do not bear a slanderous judgment against them before the LORD" (2 Pet 2:11, LSV). It omits the names of Michael and Moses and the specific story, perhaps because the story came from a non-canonical source. It also expands Jude's comparison to animals: "as irrational natural beasts, made to be caught and destroyed" (2 Pet 2:12, LSV).

**Daniel 10:13, 21; 12:1; Revelation 12:7–9 (Michael).** These passages establish Michael as Israel's guardian and the leader of the heavenly forces against the dragon. Jude's portrait of a Michael who refuses to presume is all the more striking against that background.

**Matthew 4:10 (shared theme).** When Jesus dismisses Satan in the wilderness, he does so by appealing to Scripture: "it has been written" (Matt 4:10, LSV). Like Michael, he grounds his response in God's authority rather than his own.

**Romans 1:22–24; Philippians 3:19 (shared theme).** Paul's description of those who, "professing to be wise, … were made fools" (Rom 1:22, LSV) and were given up to their desires parallels Jude's portrait of people who claim higher knowledge yet live by animal appetite.

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. What is the source of the Michael story, and what does its use imply?** Discussed in the deep dive. The source is most likely a lost ending of the *Testament* or *Assumption of Moses*. Christians have drawn different conclusions about what Jude's use of the story implies about its historical truth.

**2. What is "lordship," and who are the "glorious ones"?** Discussed above. The most likely readings are the Lord's authority and angels, respectively. Alternatives are noted.

**3. What does "a judgment of slander" mean?** "A slanderous judgment" (traditional) or "a condemnation for slander" (reconstructed setting). Both lead to the same point.

**4. Should Christians "rebuke the Devil"?** Some modern Christian practices encourage believers to address and rebuke Satan directly in prayer. Jude 9 has been cited on both sides of this question. Some read it as a model, since Michael's words are a form of rebuke spoken in God's name. Others read it as a caution, since Michael refused to pronounce judgment himself and appealed to God. Jude's own point concerns *slander and presumption*, not techniques of spiritual warfare. The verse most clearly teaches humility: whatever believers say about spiritual evil should be said in dependence on God's authority, not their own.

## H. The Passage Within Jude

Verse 8 is the first application in the letter's alternating pattern of text and application. Verses 5–7 supplied the text, and v. 8 says "In like manner … those dreaming." Verse 9 then introduces a second "text," the Michael story, and v. 10 applies it: "But these…"

The passage also prepares for what follows. The animal comparison of v. 10 anticipates the woe on those who follow Cain, Balaam, and Korah (v. 11). The theme of slanderous speech anticipates the "harsh [words]" of v. 15 and the "swollen words" of v. 16. The phrase "they understand naturally" anticipates the description of the intruders in v. 19 as ψυχικοί (*psychikoi*), "natural" people "not having the Spirit." In Jude's view, the intruders' claimed spirituality is in fact the opposite of spirituality.

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**Testing claimed revelations.** Movements in every generation claim new revelations, special dreams, or private words from God that set aside the received faith or its moral teaching. Jude, like Deuteronomy and the *Didache*, suggests a test. Does the claimed revelation lead toward the faith "once delivered" and toward holiness, or away from them? Christian traditions differ on the place of ongoing prophecy in the church. They can agree that no experience authorizes a believer to "defile the flesh" or reject the lordship of Christ.

**Reverence in speech about spiritual realities.** Michael's restraint may challenge both careless mockery of spiritual realities and presumptuous bravado toward them. Christians may speak about evil truthfully and confidently, yet without contempt or self-reliance, leaving final judgment to God.

**The danger of contempt.** Jude's charge is that the intruders "speak evil of" what they do not understand. Contempt for what one has not understood is a common failing in religious and political disputes today. Jude's example invites believers to examine their own speech, including their speech about the people Jude warns against.

**Appetite and identity.** Jude's picture of people who know their appetites "like the irrational beasts" may prompt reflection on how far modern life is shaped by instinctive desire, often deliberately cultivated by commerce and media, and on how Christian discipleship offers a different kind of knowledge and freedom.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. Jude describes the intruders as "dreaming," probably meaning that they claimed revelations that justified their behavior. How can individuals and communities test claims to spiritual experience today?
2. Why do you think Jude chose a story about Michael and the Devil to make his point about slander? What would be lost if he had simply said, "Do not slander angels"?
3. Michael "did not dare" to pronounce judgment, even on the Devil. What does this suggest about the attitude believers should have toward those they consider enemies of God?
4. Jude contrasts two kinds of knowledge: ignorance of spiritual realities and instinctive knowledge of appetite. Where do you see these two kinds of "knowing" in your own life or culture?
5. How do you respond to the fact that Jude draws on a story not found in the Old Testament? Does it affect how you read the letter? Why or why not?

# Chapter 5. The Way of Cain, the Error of Balaam, the Rebellion of Korah (Jude 11)

## A. The Text

> **11** woe to them! Because they went on in the way of Cain, and they rushed to the deceit of Balaam for reward, and in the controversy of Korah they perished.
>
> — Jude 11, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

Jude now speaks as a prophet. He pronounces a "woe" over the intruders, an announcement of coming judgment mixed with lament. He gives the reason by comparing them to three notorious figures from Israel's scriptures:

- **Cain**, the first murderer, who killed his brother out of envy;
- **Balaam**, the prophet-for-hire, who led Israel into immorality and idolatry for profit;
- **Korah**, the Levite who rebelled against the leadership God had appointed and was swallowed by the earth.

Each figure stands for a kind of sin that Jude sees in the intruders. The three verbs also trace a progression: they *went* in Cain's way, they *abandoned themselves* to Balaam's error, and they *perished* in Korah's rebellion. Jude speaks of their destruction as if it had already happened, because in his view it is as certain as Korah's was.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "woe to them!"

The interjection οὐαί (*ouai*), "woe!" or "alas!", is the classic opening of a prophetic oracle of judgment. Isaiah pronounces a series of them: "Woe [to] those saying to evil 'good,' and to good 'evil'" (Isa 5:20, LSV). Habakkuk, Amos, and other prophets use the same form. Jesus pronounced woes on the rich and on the scribes and Pharisees (Luke 6:24–26; Matt 23:13–36). The Epistle of Enoch, part of 1 Enoch that Jude knew, contains long series of woes against sinners: "Woe to those who build unrighteousness and oppression / And lay deceit as a foundation; / For they shall be suddenly overthrown" (1 Enoch 94:6, trans. Charles).[^c5-1]

Outside the Gospels and Revelation, the word "woe" appears in the New Testament only twice: in Paul's "woe is to me if I may not proclaim good news" (1 Cor 9:16, LSV) and here.[^c5-2] Jude's is the only woe pronounced against others in the New Testament letters. It marks this verse as the emotional and prophetic climax of the first half of his argument.

A woe is not a curse that the speaker wishes on someone. It is a solemn announcement that judgment is coming. It often carries a note of grief. The English "alas" catches some of that tone.

### "Because they went on in the way of Cain"

The first verb, ἐπορεύθησαν (*eporeuthēsan*), "they went, walked," pictures a course of life, a "way" (ὁδός, *hodos*) followed by choice. To walk in someone's way is to follow that person's example. The phrase τῇ ὁδῷ τοῦ Κάϊν (*tē hodō tou Kain*), "the way of Cain," does not refer only to one act of murder. It refers to the kind of life Cain represents.

What did Cain represent? Genesis tells the bare story: Cain's offering was not accepted, he became angry, and he killed his brother Abel. When God asked where Abel was, Cain replied, "I have not known; am I my brother's keeper?" (Gen 4:9, LSV). The deep dive below shows how Jewish and Christian interpreters filled out the picture. For Jude's purpose, three features are most likely in view:

- **Envy and hatred of a brother.** First John draws exactly this lesson: "not as Cain—he was of the evil one, and he slew his brother. And for [what] reason did he slay him? Because his works were evil, and those of his brother [were] righteous" (1 John 3:12, LSV). Jude's intruders are divisive (v. 19), and they sit at the love feasts while betraying their meaning (v. 12).
- **Cynicism about judgment.** Jewish tradition remembered Cain as the first person to deny that God judges. In the Targum's retelling, Cain says to Abel, "There is neither judgment nor Judge, nor another world; nor will good reward be given to the righteous, nor vengeance be taken of the wicked."[^c5-3] Jude's intruders, the "scoffers" of v. 18, live as if judgment were not real.
- **Corrupting others.** Josephus describes Cain after his exile as one who "excited his acquaintance to procure pleasures and spoils by robbery, and became a great leader of men into wicked courses."[^c5-4] Jude's intruders lead others astray.

Jude does not say which aspect he means. He may intend the whole tradition. Cain is the first person in Scripture to turn against God's order and against his brother, the father of a "way" that others follow.

### "and they rushed to the deceit of Balaam for reward"

The second verb is vivid. ἐξεχύθησαν (*exechythēsan*) is the passive of ἐκχέω (*ekcheō*), "to pour out." Used of people it means "they were poured out," that is, they gave themselves up wholly or abandoned themselves.[^c5-5] The LSV's "rushed" catches the headlong motion. The ASV has "ran riotously," the KJV "ran greedily," and the BSB "rushed headlong." The intruders did not drift into Balaam's error. They threw themselves into it.

"The deceit of Balaam" translates τῇ πλάνῃ τοῦ Βαλαάμ (*tē planē tou Balaam*). The noun πλάνη (*planē*) means "error, wandering, deception," the state of going astray and leading others astray. It belongs to the same word family as the "stars going astray" (ἀστέρες πλανῆται, *asteres planētai*) of v. 13.

The phrase μισθοῦ (*misthou*), "for reward" or "for pay," is a genitive of price: they abandoned themselves to error *for wages*. Balaam was famous for being hired. Moab's king Balak sent elders "with divinations in their hand," payment for the seer's services (Num 22:7, LSV), and promised to honor him greatly (Num 22:17). Deuteronomy remembers that Moab "hired against you Balaam son of Beor … to revile you" (Deut 23:4, LSV). Second Peter summarizes: Balaam "loved a reward of unrighteousness" (2 Pet 2:15, LSV).

Balaam's "error" was more than greed. According to Numbers, after he failed to curse Israel, it was "through the word of Balaam" that the Israelites were led "to cause a trespass against YHWH in the matter of Peor" (Num 31:16, LSV), the episode in which Israelite men engaged in sexual immorality with Moabite and Midianite women and joined in the worship of their gods (Num 25:1–3). Revelation puts the tradition plainly: Balaam "taught Balak to cast a stumbling-block before the sons of Israel, to eat idol-sacrifices, and to commit whoredom" (Rev 2:14, LSV). For Jude's readers, Balaam was the prophet who, for profit, taught God's people that immorality was permissible. The parallel with the intruders, who turn grace into licentiousness (v. 4) and flatter people "for the sake of profit" (v. 16), is exact.

### "and in the controversy of Korah they perished"

The third clause turns to rebellion. "Controversy" translates ἀντιλογίᾳ (*antilogia*), literally "speaking against," hence "contradiction, dispute, rebellion."[^c5-6] The LSV's "controversy" is literal. Most translations have "rebellion" (WEB, BSB) or "gainsaying" (ASV, KJV).

The word carries an echo that Jude's Greek-reading audience may have heard. In the Septuagint, ἀντιλογία is the word used for the "water of contention" at Meribah, where Israel quarreled with Moses and God: Τοῦτο τὸ ὕδωρ Ἀντιλογίας, ‘This is the water of Contention’ (Num 20:13 LXX; also Num 27:14; Deut 32:51; 33:8). It is also used for legal disputes brought before judges (Exod 18:16; Deut 17:8; 19:17).[^c5-7] The word suggests a challenge to God-given authority.

Korah's story is told in Numbers 16. A Levite, already privileged to serve at the tabernacle, he led two hundred and fifty leaders of the congregation against Moses and Aaron. His slogan sounded spiritual and egalitarian: "Enough of you! For all the congregation—all of them [are] holy, and YHWH [is] in their midst; and why do you lift yourselves up above the assembly of YHWH?" (Num 16:3, LSV). Moses saw through it: "is it little to you that the God of Israel has separated you … and you have also sought the priesthood! Therefore, you and all your congregation are meeting against YHWH" (Num 16:9–11, LSV). The judgment was spectacular: "the earth opens her mouth and swallows them … and they go down—they and all that they have—alive to Sheol … and they perish from the midst of the assembly" (Num 16:32–33, LSV), while fire consumed the two hundred and fifty who offered incense (16:35).

The verb ἀπώλοντο (*apōlonto*), "they perished," is in the aorist, a past tense. Jude speaks of the intruders as if they had *already* perished. Grammarians sometimes call this a "prophetic" use of the past tense: a future event is described as already accomplished because it is so certain. In a sense they perished when they joined Korah's rebellion, as Korah's company was doomed the moment it set itself against God. The word ἀπόλλυμι (*apollymi*) also links back to v. 5, where Jesus "destroyed" (ἀπώλεσεν, *apōlesen*) the unbelieving wilderness generation.

### The progression of the three verbs

Read in sequence, the three verbs describe a downward path:

| Verb | Figure | Stage |
|---|---|---|
| ἐπορεύθησαν, "they went" | Cain | Setting out on a way of life |
| ἐξεχύθησαν, "they rushed / abandoned themselves" | Balaam | Throwing oneself into it wholeheartedly |
| ἀπώλοντο, "they perished" | Korah | Reaching its end |

The sins also escalate. Cain's sin was personal: hatred, murder, and contempt for judgment. Balaam's sin led others into sin for profit. Korah's sin openly defied God's appointed order. The intruders, Jude suggests, embody all three.

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| οὐαί | *ouai* | woe, alas | "woe" | Prophetic oracle of judgment; only woe against others in the NT letters. |
| πορεύομαι | *poreuomai* | go, walk, proceed | "went on" | Way of life; also vv. 16, 18. |
| ὁδός | *hodos* | way, road | "the way of Cain" | Pattern of life. |
| Κάϊν | *Kain* | Cain | "Cain" | |
| ἐκχέω (pass.) | *ekcheō* | pour out; (of persons) abandon oneself | "rushed" | Passive used reflexively. |
| πλάνη | *planē* | error, deception, wandering | "deceit" | Related to πλανῆται (v. 13). |
| Βαλαάμ | *Balaam* | Balaam | "Balaam" | |
| μισθός | *misthos* | wages, reward | "for reward" | Genitive of price. |
| ἀντιλογία | *antilogia* | contradiction, dispute, rebellion | "controversy" | LXX: Meribah "water of contention"; legal disputes. |
| Κόρε | *Kore* | Korah | "Korah" | Only here in the NT. |
| ἀπόλλυμι | *apollymi* | destroy; (mid.) perish | "they perished" | Aorist describing certain doom; links to v. 5. |

**A caution about "prophetic aorist."** Not every past tense in prophecy is a "prophetic aorist." Here, though, the context makes the category apt. Jude has just said the intruders' judgment is "written beforehand" (v. 4) and is about to quote Enoch's prophecy of judgment in the past tense: "Behold, the LORD has come" (v. 14). The past tense expresses certainty.

### Deep dive: Cain, Balaam, and Korah in Scripture and tradition

The three figures had rich afterlives in Jewish and Christian interpretation, and Jude's readers would have heard his brief allusions against that background.

**Cain.**

- *In Genesis* (4:1–16), Cain is the firstborn of Adam and Eve and a tiller of the ground. His offering is not accepted. God warns him that sin is crouching at the door but that he must rule over it (4:7). He kills Abel, denies responsibility, and is sentenced to be "a wanderer, even a trembling one" (4:12, LSV), though God marks him for protection.
- *In the New Testament*, Cain is the archetype of hatred: "not as Cain—he was of the evil one" (1 John 3:12, LSV). Hebrews contrasts Abel's faith with Cain's lack of it: "By faith Abel offered a better sacrifice to God than Cain" (Heb 11:4, LSV). Jesus speaks of "all the righteous blood … from the blood of Abel the righteous" (Matt 23:35, LSV).
- *In Jewish tradition*, Cain became the prototype of several vices. Josephus portrays him as "wholly intent upon getting," the inventor of "measures and weights," who "changed the world into cunning craftiness" and "became a great leader of men into wicked courses."[^c5-8] The Palestinian Targums turn the brothers' conversation in the field into a theological argument, with Cain denying that there is "judgment" or a "Judge" or "another world," and Abel affirming them, before Cain kills him.[^c5-9] (The Targums as we have them were written down later than Jude, though they preserve older traditions. Their witness here is to a line of interpretation, not proof of what Jude had read.)

Any of these portraits could stand behind Jude's "way of Cain." Jealous hatred fits the divisions in v. 19. Leading others into sin and greed fit vv. 11–12 and 16. Denial of judgment fits the "scoffers" of v. 18. Jude's brevity leaves the allusion open, and that openness allows it to cover the full range of the intruders' behavior.

**Balaam.**

- *In Numbers 22–24*, Balaam is a non-Israelite seer from Pethor on the Euphrates whom Balak, king of Moab, hires to curse Israel. Repeatedly, he insists, "If Balak gives to me the fullness of his house of silver and gold, I am not able to pass over the command of YHWH" (Num 24:13, LSV), and he ends up blessing Israel. His donkey sees the angel that he cannot see and speaks to him (22:21–35). Read by itself, the story is ambiguous. Balaam obeys God's word in the end, but his eagerness to go, and God's anger at his going (22:22), suggest mixed motives.
- *The rest of Scripture* resolves the ambiguity negatively. Numbers 31:16 blames Balaam's counsel for the apostasy at Peor. Deuteronomy, Nehemiah, and Joshua remember him as hired to curse Israel and as "the diviner" killed among Israel's enemies (Deut 23:4–5; Neh 13:2; Josh 13:22). Micah calls Israel to "remember … What Balaam son of Beor answered" Balak (Mic 6:5, LSV).
- *In Jewish tradition*, Josephus has Balaam advise the Midianites to send their most beautiful daughters to the Israelite camp to seduce the young men into abandoning their law and worshipping other gods, "for by this means God will be angry at them."[^c5-10]
- *In the New Testament*, Balaam becomes the type of the false teacher who is motivated by money and leads believers into immorality and idolatry (2 Pet 2:15–16; Rev 2:14).

Jude's two words, "error" and "reward," compress this whole tradition. The intruders are teachers who, for personal gain, mislead God's people into sexual immorality while claiming spiritual authority.

**Korah.**

- *In Numbers 16*, Korah, a Levite of the clan of Kohath, joined with the Reubenites Dathan, Abiram, and On to challenge Moses' leadership and Aaron's priesthood. God vindicated Moses: the earth swallowed the rebels, and fire consumed the two hundred and fifty incense-offerers. Afterward, God confirmed Aaron's priesthood by making his rod blossom, so that "the murmurings of the sons of Israel" would cease (Num 17:5, LSV).
- *Elsewhere in Scripture*, Korah's rebellion is remembered as "striving against YHWH" (Num 26:9, LSV) and as envy: "they are envious of Moses in the camp, / Of Aaron, YHWH's holy one" (Ps 106:16, LSV). Ben Sira recalls how "the congregation of Core" maligned Aaron "with fury and wrath" and were "consumed … with the fiery flame" (Sir 45:18–19, Brenton). Remarkably, "the sons of Korah did not die" (Num 26:11, LSV). Their descendants became temple singers, and several psalms bear their name.
- *In Jewish tradition*, Korah became the archetype of the schismatic who challenges legitimate authority under the guise of spiritual equality.

Korah's slogan, "all the congregation—all of them [are] holy," sounds like something the New Testament itself might say, and in a sense it does (1 Pet 2:9). That is what makes him a fitting type for the intruders. Their rejection of authority (v. 8) may have been dressed in the language of spiritual freedom and equality. Korah shows that true statements can be used to justify rebellion against God's order.

**Why these three?** All three are figures who opposed God's purposes while associated in some way with God's people or God's word: a son of Adam, a prophet, and a Levite. All three led others, or tried to lead others, with them. All three met judgment. Together they show that the intruders' danger lies not only in their own sin but in their influence on others.

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Prophetic speech in the early church.** Early Christian communities recognized prophets alongside apostles and teachers (Acts 13:1; 1 Cor 12:28; Eph 4:11). Jude's woe oracle shows him speaking with prophetic authority, in the tradition of Israel's prophets and of Jesus. His opponents, the "dreamers," may also have claimed prophetic gifts. Jude answers prophecy with prophecy, but his prophecy is anchored in Scripture.

**Paid religious teachers.** In the Greco-Roman world, philosophers and religious specialists often taught for fees, and critics of the time routinely mocked them for greed. Early Christians debated how traveling ministers should be supported. Paul defended the right to support but often declined it so as not to burden or mislead (1 Cor 9:3–18; 1 Thess 2:5–9). The *Didache* warns that a traveling apostle who "ask[s] money" is a false prophet.[^c5-11] Balaam was a fitting type for teachers whose message bent to their income.

**Interpreting Israel's stories as types.** Jewish and early Christian teachers commonly read figures from Israel's history as patterns (Greek τύποι, *typoi*, "types") for the present. Paul says of the wilderness stories, "these things happened to those persons as types, and they were written for our admonition" (1 Cor 10:11, LSV). Jude's three figures are used in exactly this way.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**1 John 3:11–12 (shared tradition).** John's use of Cain as the antithesis of brotherly love is the closest New Testament parallel to Jude's reference. Both writers assume that their readers know Cain as a type of the person who belongs to the evil one.

**2 Peter 2:15–16 (literary parallel).** Peter expands Jude's Balaam reference, mentioning Balaam's love of "a reward of unrighteousness" and the "mute donkey" that "forbid the madness of the prophet" (LSV). He omits Cain and Korah.

**Revelation 2:14 (shared tradition).** The letter to Pergamum names "those holding the teaching of Balaam," which Revelation defines as eating idol-sacrifices and committing sexual immorality. The parallel shows that Balaam had become a standard type of the antinomian teacher in early Christianity.

**Numbers 16; Psalm 106:16–18; Sirach 45:18–19 (Korah).** The core narrative and its later echoes. The theme of envy in Ps 106:16 parallels the grumbling of Jude 16.

**Isaiah 5; Luke 6:24–26; Matthew 23 (woes).** The prophetic and dominical woes provide the form of Jude's oracle.

**1 Corinthians 10:1–11 (method).** Paul's typological reading of the wilderness narratives, including the warning "neither murmur, as also some of them murmured, and perished by the destroyer" (1 Cor 10:10, LSV), parallels Jude's method and some of his material.

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. Which aspect of Cain does Jude intend?** Interpreters differ. Some emphasize hatred and murder (following 1 John), some the corruption of others (following Josephus), and some the denial of judgment (following the Targums). Bauckham and others have argued that Jude draws on the Jewish tradition of Cain as the archetypal sinner and corrupter of others, which fits the context of false teaching.[^c5-12] The allusion may be deliberately broad.

**2. Is the aorist "they perished" prophetic?** Most commentators take it so. An alternative view holds that Jude regards the intruders' fate as already sealed by their choice, in the way Korah's company was doomed as soon as it set itself against Moses.

**3. How does Jude's use of Balaam relate to Numbers 22–24?** Numbers 22–24 portrays Balaam ambiguously, and other passages (Num 31; Deut 23; Neh 13; Josh 13) portray him negatively. Jude relies on the latter. This shows that the New Testament writers read individual passages in light of the whole canon and of traditional interpretation.

**4. Is authority itself sacred?** Jude's use of Korah could be misused to defend any human authority against criticism. Moses and Aaron's authority in Numbers 16 was explicitly God-given and confirmed by God. Korah's sin was rebellion against *God's* appointment, not criticism of human leaders as such. The Bible elsewhere honors prophets who challenged corrupt leaders (Nathan, Elijah, Jeremiah). Applying Jude 11 requires discernment about whether authority is truly from God and exercised under God.

## H. The Passage Within Jude

Verse 11 is the climax of the section that began in v. 8. The "dreamers" of v. 8, who slander what they do not understand and are ruled by instinct (v. 10), are now placed in a line of biblical rebels. The woe also prepares for the dense metaphors of vv. 12–13, which open "These are…" and describe the intruders' character and fate. The three verbs of v. 11, *went*, *rushed*, and *perished*, recall the movement of the earlier examples, from salvation to destruction (v. 5).

The three figures also connect with later verses. Balaam's "reward" anticipates the intruders' flattery "for the sake of profit" (v. 16). Korah's "murmuring" (Num 16:11; 17:5) anticipates the "discontented grumblers" of v. 16. Cain's "way" anticipates their "walking according to their own lusts" (vv. 16, 18).

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**Three enduring temptations.** Cain, Balaam, and Korah represent temptations that recur in every religious community: resentment and hatred toward fellow believers; using spiritual influence for personal gain; and rejecting God's order while using spiritual language to justify it. Jude's woe may invite communities to examine themselves before they examine others.

**Religion and money.** Balaam's "reward" remains a live issue. Churches and ministries today face questions about how leaders are supported, how money shapes messages, and how marketing can bend teaching. Jude does not condemn all payment for ministry; other New Testament writers defend it. He does condemn error embraced for profit.

**Equality as a cover for rebellion.** Korah's slogan, "all the congregation—all of them [are] holy," was true and yet was used to justify defiance of God's appointment. Believers may need discernment to recognize when good principles are being used for self-promotion, while remaining open to legitimate challenges to leaders who abuse their position.

**The weight of influence.** All three figures harmed others. Teachers, parents, leaders, and anyone with influence may consider that their "way" becomes a path others walk.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. Why do you think Jude chose a "woe" rather than a simple warning? How does the form of a prophetic oracle change the effect of his words?
2. Which of the three figures (Cain, Balaam, Korah) seems most relevant to the challenges facing Christians today? Why?
3. Korah's slogan was that "all the congregation" is holy. How can a community tell the difference between a legitimate challenge to leaders and a Korah-like rebellion?
4. Balaam's error was embraced "for reward." In what subtle ways can financial or social rewards shape what religious leaders say, or what congregations want to hear?
5. Jude's three verbs describe a downward progression: going, rushing, perishing. Where might a person or community notice the early stages of such a path, and turn back?

## K. Sources
