# Chapter 10. Remember What the Apostles Said (Jude 17–19)

## A. The Text

> **17** but you, beloved, remember the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ, **18** that they said to you, that in the last time there will be scoffers, following after their own lusts of impieties, **19** these are those causing divisions, natural men, not having the Spirit.
>
> — Jude 17–19, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

With "but you, beloved," Jude turns from the intruders to his readers. He has been building his case from ancient sources: Israel's history, angelic traditions, the stories of Cain, Balaam, and Korah, and Enoch's prophecy. Now he brings it close to home. The readers themselves had heard the apostles warn that in the last days there would be scoffers, driven by ungodly desires. The intruders fulfill that prediction. In Jude's final description of them, they are people who create divisions, who are merely "natural" in their outlook, and who do not have the Spirit.

This is the last "text and application" unit in the letter. Its text is not an ancient prophecy but the recent teaching of the apostles, and its application is the most theologically pointed charge of all: the people who claim spiritual revelation do not have the Spirit.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "but you, beloved"

The Greek Ὑμεῖς δέ, ἀγαπητοί (*Hymeis de, agapētoi*) places the pronoun "you" first for emphasis: "*But you*, beloved." It marks a sharp contrast with the repeated "these" (οὗτοι, *houtoi*) of the previous verses. This is the second of Jude's three addresses to the "beloved" (vv. 3, 17, 20), and it begins the turn toward the readers that will carry through to the end of the letter.

### "remember"

The aorist imperative μνήσθητε (*mnēsthēte*), "remember," is Jude's first direct command to his readers. In the Bible, remembering is never merely recalling information. It means calling the past into the present so that it shapes action. Moses tells Israel, "you have remembered all the way which your God YHWH has caused you to go these forty years in the wilderness, in order to humble you" (Deut 8:2, LSV). Paul writes, "Remember Jesus Christ, raised out of the dead" (2 Tim 2:8, LSV). Jude began the main body of his letter with "I intend to remind you" (v. 5). Now he asks his readers to do their own remembering.

### "the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ"

The readers are to remember τῶν ῥημάτων τῶν προειρημένων ὑπὸ τῶν ἀποστόλων (*tōn rhēmatōn tōn proeirēmenōn hypo tōn apostolōn*), "the words spoken beforehand by the apostles." The perfect participle προειρημένων (*proeirēmenōn*), "spoken before" or "foretold," stresses that the warning was given in advance and still stands.

Two observations follow:

1. **Jude distinguishes himself from "the apostles."** He does not say "we apostles." This fits the author's modest self-description in v. 1 and is one of the reasons for thinking that the author did not claim apostolic status. Some scholars have read the phrase as evidence that the apostles belonged to a past generation and that the letter is therefore late. The next verse suggests otherwise: the apostles had spoken "to you," to the readers themselves.
2. **The apostles' words are placed alongside the prophets.** Jude has cited Israel's history and Enoch's prophecy. Now he adds the apostles' teaching as a further authority. Second Peter's parallel makes the pairing explicit: "to be mindful of the sayings said before by the holy prophets, and of the command of us the apostles of the LORD and Savior" (2 Pet 3:2, LSV). For the early church, the prophets and the apostles together were the foundation of the faith (Eph 2:20).

### "that they said to you"

The imperfect ἔλεγον (*elegon*), "they were saying" or "they used to say," suggests repeated teaching rather than a single statement. The warning about scoffers was a regular part of apostolic instruction. And it was said ὑμῖν (*hymin*), "to you." Jude's readers had heard it firsthand, probably from the missionaries who founded their community. That detail points to a date within the first generation or so of the church. It also makes Jude's appeal personal. He is not citing a distant authority. He is reminding them of what they themselves heard.

What did the apostles say? The New Testament preserves several such warnings. Paul told the Ephesian elders: "there will enter in, after my departing, grievous wolves to you, not sparing the flock, and there will arise men of your own selves, speaking perverse things, to draw away the disciples after them" (Acts 20:29–30, LSV). He told Timothy that "in latter times some will depart from the faith" (1 Tim 4:1, LSV) and that "in the last days there will come perilous times, for men will be lovers of themselves, lovers of money, boasters, proud, slanderous … unthankful … lovers of pleasure more than lovers of God, having a form of piety, but having denied its power" (2 Tim 3:1–5, LSV). Jesus himself had warned: "many false prophets will arise, and will lead many astray" (Matt 24:11, LSV); "behold, I have foretold you all things" (Mark 13:23, LSV). Jude's summary does not quote any of these passages directly, but it reflects the same tradition of prophetic warning.

### "that in the last time there will be scoffers"

The phrase ἐπ' ἐσχάτου χρόνου (*ep' eschatou chronou*), "at the last time," reflects the conviction, shared by the whole New Testament, that the coming of Christ inaugurated the "last days." Hebrews says God has spoken "in these last days … in [His] Son" (Heb 1:2, LSV). Peter at Pentecost quoted Joel's promise of what would happen "in the last days" (Acts 2:17). John says, "Little youths, it is the last hour" (1 John 2:18, LSV). The "last time" is not a distant future. It is the period in which the church lives, between Christ's first coming and his return.

**A textual note.** The manuscripts differ in the wording of this phrase. The Byzantine text and TR read ἐν ἐσχάτῳ χρόνῳ (*en eschatō chronō*), "in the last time." NA27 read ἐπ' ἐσχάτου τοῦ χρόνου, with the article. SBLGNT and NA28 read ἐπ' ἐσχάτου χρόνου. Some manuscripts also add a second ὅτι ("that") before the quotation. The sense is unaffected.[^c10-1]

The **scoffers** (ἐμπαῖκται, *empaiktai*) are "mockers," people who ridicule. The word appears in the New Testament only here and in 2 Pet 3:3.[^c10-2] In 2 Peter the scoffers mock the promise of Christ's return: "Where is the promise of His coming? For since the fathers fell asleep, all things so remain from the beginning of the creation" (2 Pet 3:4, LSV). Jude does not specify the object of their mockery. In the context of his letter it is most naturally the moral demands of the faith and the warning of judgment. The intruders treat God's judgment as a joke, and their way of life follows from it. The "scorner" of Israel's wisdom literature is a similar figure: "O the blessedness of that one, … And has not sat in the seat of scorners" (Ps 1:1, LSV). Proverbs adds, "Do not reprove a scorner, lest he hate you" (Prov 9:8, LSV).

### "following after their own lusts of impieties"

The scoffers are κατὰ τὰς ἑαυτῶν ἐπιθυμίας πορευόμενοι τῶν ἀσεβειῶν (*kata tas heautōn epithymias poreuomenoi tōn asebeiōn*), literally "going according to their own desires of ungodlinesses." The genitive "of impieties" describes the kind of desires: ungodly desires. The phrase echoes v. 16 almost word for word ("walking according to their own lusts") and adds Jude's key word one last time: ἀσέβεια, "impiety." From v. 4, through the fourfold repetition in the Enoch quotation (v. 15), to here, the intruders are consistently the "ungodly."

### "these are those causing divisions"

The final application begins with the familiar formula, Οὗτοί εἰσιν (*Houtoi eisin*), "These are." The first description is οἱ ἀποδιορίζοντες (*hoi apodiorizontes*). The verb, found only here in the New Testament, means "to mark off, separate, make distinctions."[^c10-3] It is built from ὅρος (*horos*, "boundary") with prefixes meaning "away" and "through." The intruders draw lines through the community.

Two senses are possible:

1. **"Those who cause divisions."** The intruders create factions, separating their followers from the rest of the community. Paul warns against "those who are causing the divisions and the stumbling-blocks, contrary to the teaching that you learned" (Rom 16:17, LSV), and lists "dissensions, sects" among the works of the flesh (Gal 5:20, LSV).
2. **"Those who make distinctions."** The intruders classify people into spiritual ranks, regarding themselves as the truly "spiritual" and others as inferior. This reading fits the next word, ψυχικοί, especially if that was a term the intruders used for others.

The two senses overlap. Drawing distinctions of spiritual rank is a way of dividing a community. The Textus Receptus adds ἑαυτούς (*heautous*), "themselves," which gives the KJV's "These be they who separate themselves," as though the intruders withdrew from the community. Most manuscripts and the critical editions lack the word.[^c10-4] Jude's picture is not of people who have left but of people who divide from within. They still sit at the love feasts (v. 12).

### "natural men"

The adjective ψυχικοί (*psychikoi*) is difficult to translate. It derives from ψυχή (*psychē*), "soul, life," and describes what belongs to ordinary, natural human life as opposed to what belongs to the Spirit of God.[^c10-5] The LSV has "natural men," the KJV "sensual," the WEB "worldly-minded," and the BSB "worldly."

Paul uses the word in a similar contrast: "the natural man does not receive the things of the Spirit of God, for they are foolishness to him … and he who is spiritual, indeed discerns all things" (1 Cor 2:14–15, LSV). He also contrasts the "natural body" with the "spiritual body" of the resurrection (1 Cor 15:44). James describes a false wisdom as "earthly, physical, demon-like" (Jas 3:15, LSV), where "physical" translates the same Greek word.

The term carries a sharp irony. If the intruders claimed spiritual superiority through their dreams and visions, and perhaps used "natural" (*psychikoi*) as a label for less enlightened believers, Jude turns their own category back on them. *They* are the merely natural ones. A century later, some Gnostic groups divided humanity into "spiritual," "natural" (*psychikoi*), and "material" classes. Jude's opponents were not second-century Gnostics, and it would be anachronistic to read that system into the letter. The Corinthian correspondence shows, however, that the contrast between "natural" and "spiritual" people was already being used in early Christian disputes about status.

### "not having the Spirit"

The final phrase, πνεῦμα μὴ ἔχοντες (*pneuma mē echontes*), "not having the Spirit," is the most serious charge in the letter. In the New Testament, possessing the Holy Spirit is the mark of belonging to Christ. Paul writes: "if anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ—this one is not His" (Rom 8:9, LSV), and "in one Spirit we were all immersed into one body" (1 Cor 12:13, LSV).

The intruders, who appear to have claimed special spiritual experiences, are in Jude's judgment devoid of the Spirit. Their dreams (v. 8) were not the Spirit's gift. Their conduct, with its immorality, greed, grumbling, arrogance, and divisiveness, is the opposite of the fruit of the Spirit. The very next verse urges the readers to pray "in the Holy Spirit" (v. 20). Jude's contrast is complete: the intruders lack the Spirit, while the readers pray in the Spirit.

(The Greek word πνεῦμα has no article here and could in principle mean "spirit" in a general sense: "not having a spirit," that is, being merely soulish and lacking a spiritual dimension. Given the New Testament usage and the contrast with v. 20, most interpreters take it as the Holy Spirit.)

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ὑμεῖς δέ | *hymeis de* | but you | "but you" | Emphatic contrast with "these." |
| μιμνῄσκομαι | *mimnēskomai* | remember | "remember" | Aorist imperative. |
| ῥῆμα | *rhēma* | word, saying | "the sayings" | |
| προλέγω | *prolegō* | say beforehand, foretell | "spoken before" | Perfect passive participle. |
| ἀπόστολος | *apostolos* | apostle, sent one | "the apostles" | Jude does not include himself. |
| ἔλεγον | *elegon* | they were saying | "they said" | Imperfect: repeated teaching. |
| ἔσχατος χρόνος | *eschatos chronos* | last time | "the last time" | Variants in wording. |
| ἐμπαίκτης | *empaiktēs* | mocker, scoffer | "scoffers" | Only here and 2 Pet 3:3. |
| ἐπιθυμία | *epithymia* | desire | "lusts" | Repeats v. 16. |
| ἀσέβεια | *asebeia* | impiety | "impieties" | Final use of the key word. |
| ἀποδιορίζω | *apodiorizō* | mark off, make divisions | "causing divisions" | NT hapax. TR adds ἑαυτούς. |
| ψυχικός | *psychikos* | natural, soulish, unspiritual | "natural men" | 1 Cor 2:14; 15:44; Jas 3:15. |
| πνεῦμα | *pneuma* | spirit, Spirit | "the Spirit" | The Holy Spirit (cf. v. 20). |

**A caution about ψυχικός.** It is tempting to build a doctrine of human nature, body, soul, and spirit, on words like ψυχικός. That is not Jude's purpose, nor Paul's in 1 Corinthians. The word describes an *orientation*: life lived on the level of ordinary human impulses, without the transforming presence of God's Spirit. It does not mean that the soul is bad or that the Spirit replaces human nature.

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Apostolic teaching and its transmission.** In the first decades of the church, the apostles and their associates traveled among the churches teaching, and their teaching was passed on orally before and alongside written letters. Jude's readers had heard apostolic warnings "said to you." The phrase gives a glimpse of the early process by which communities received, remembered, and relied on the apostles' words, the same process that eventually led to the collection of the New Testament writings.

**Factions and spiritual elitism.** The churches of the first century were small communities in which differences of background, status, and spiritual experience could easily harden into factions. The Corinthian church divided around favored teachers ("I, indeed, am of Paul," 1 Cor 3:4, LSV) and around spiritual gifts (1 Cor 12–14). Jude's intruders seem to have created a similar division, with their followers presented as the truly spiritual.

**"Last days" expectation.** Jewish apocalyptic thought expected a period of intensified evil and apostasy before God's final intervention. Early Christians believed that this period had begun with Christ, and they interpreted false teaching and moral collapse within the church as signs of it (1 John 2:18–19; 2 Tim 3:1–5). Jude shares that outlook.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**2 Peter 3:1–4 (literary parallel).** Peter's version of this passage pairs the prophets and the apostles and specifies that the scoffers mock the promise of Christ's coming. The relationship between the two letters is especially close here.

**Acts 20:29–30; 1 Timothy 4:1; 2 Timothy 3:1–5; 4:3–4 (apostolic predictions).** These passages illustrate the kind of warning Jude's readers had heard. Note especially 2 Tim 4:3: people "will heap up teachers according to their own desires" (LSV).

**Matthew 24:11, 24; Mark 13:22–23 (dominical predictions).** Jesus' warnings about false prophets in the last days are the root of the apostolic tradition.

**1 John 2:18–19 (shared situation).** John's opponents "went forth out of us, but they were not of us" (1 John 2:19, LSV). Jude's intruders, by contrast, are still within the community, causing division from inside.

**1 Corinthians 2:14–15; 15:44–46; James 3:15 (vocabulary).** The contrast between the "natural" and the "spiritual."

**Romans 8:9; 1 Corinthians 12:13 (theology of the Spirit).** Having the Spirit is the mark of belonging to Christ.

**Romans 16:17; 1 Corinthians 1:10; 3:3–4; Galatians 5:20 (divisions).** Paul's warnings against division illuminate Jude's charge.

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. Does v. 17 show that Jude is late?** Some argue that the reference to "the apostles" as a group whose words are remembered implies a post-apostolic date. Others point out that v. 18 says the apostles spoke "to you," the readers, which places the letter within the lifetime of people who had heard the apostles. The verse is compatible with an early or a later date, but it does not require a late one.

**2. Which apostles?** Jude does not name them. The readers may have been evangelized by Paul, by Peter, or by other apostles and missionaries. Since the warning is generic, the question cannot be settled.

**3. Did the intruders use the term ψυχικοί?** It is an attractive hypothesis, especially given the parallel in 1 Corinthians, but it cannot be proven from Jude alone. What is certain is that Jude uses the term to deny the intruders' spiritual claims.

**4. Can someone in the church "not have the Spirit"?** Jude's judgment raises a pastoral and theological question. Christian traditions differ on how to understand people who are outwardly part of the church but show no evidence of the Spirit's work. Some understand them as never having been truly regenerate. Others see them as having fallen away, and others again allow for degrees of unfaithfulness. Jude does not offer a theory. His purpose is to warn his readers not to mistake the intruders' claims for spiritual reality. Notably, he still holds out hope of rescue for those influenced by them (vv. 22–23).

## H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 17–19 complete the long central section that began in v. 5. They form the fifth "text and application" unit, and the pattern of the whole section is now visible:

| Text | Application |
|---|---|
| Three examples of judgment (vv. 5–7) | "those dreaming…" (v. 8) |
| Michael and the Devil (v. 9) | "these … speak evil" (v. 10) |
| Cain, Balaam, Korah (v. 11) | "These are stains…" (vv. 12–13) |
| Enoch's prophecy (vv. 14–15) | "These are … grumblers" (v. 16) |
| The apostles' prediction (vv. 17–18) | "these are those causing divisions" (v. 19) |

The texts move from the distant past to the recent past, from the exodus to the readers' own memory of the apostles. The last application is the climax: the intruders lack the Spirit. That sets up the contrast with which the next section opens: "But you, beloved, … praying in the Holy Spirit" (v. 20).

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**The discipline of remembering.** Jude does not tell his readers to figure out new strategies. He tells them to remember. Christian communities today may find stability in recalling what they have received, in Scripture, creeds, and the teaching of faithful predecessors, rather than in perpetual novelty.

**Warnings as gifts.** The apostles warned their converts about future false teachers. Warning is an act of love. Leaders and parents who prepare others for difficulty are doing apostolic work.

**Spiritual elitism.** The intruders seem to have divided the community into the spiritual and the merely natural. Every era has its versions: groups that claim superior experience, knowledge, or holiness and look down on ordinary believers. Jude's verdict, that such dividers may themselves be "natural" and "not having the Spirit," is a warning against spiritual pride in every form, including in those who oppose the intruders.

**The evidence of the Spirit.** Jude's charge that the intruders lack the Spirit invites a question for every believer: what is the evidence of the Spirit's presence? The New Testament answers with love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, self-control (Gal 5:22–23), unity (Eph 4:3), and prayer (Jude 20), rather than with dramatic experiences alone.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. Jude tells his readers to "remember." What specific teachings or warnings do you think Christians today most need to remember?
2. The apostles warned that "scoffers" would come. What forms does scoffing at faith take today, both outside and inside the church?
3. How can a community distinguish between healthy diversity and divisive factionalism?
4. What does it mean to "have the Spirit"? How would you recognize the Spirit's presence in a person or community?
5. Spiritual elitism divided Jude's community. Where might spiritual pride show up in your own context, perhaps even among those who consider themselves defenders of the faith?

## K. Sources

- LSV texts: eBible.org edition. Greek: SBLGNT and apparatus (NA27/NA28 data); TR (ἑαυτούς in v. 19).
- Abbott-Smith, s.vv. ἐμπαίκτης, ἀποδιορίζω, ψυχικός.
- Clement of Alexandria, *Adumbrationes* on Jude 19, ANF 2.
- Word counts: MorphGNT/SBLGNT.

[^c10-1]: SBLGNT apparatus and eBible.org SBLGNT notes: "ὑμῖν WH NA28 ] + ὅτι Treg NA27 RP • Ἐπʼ ἐσχάτου χρόνου WH Treg NA28 ] Ἐπʼ ἐσχάτου τοῦ χρόνου NA27; ἐν ἐσχάτῳ χρόνῳ RP."

[^c10-2]: Computed from MorphGNT/SBLGNT. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἐμπαίκτης: "a mocker: II Pe 3:3, Ju 18," noting one LXX occurrence (Isa 3:4).

[^c10-3]: Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἀποδιορίζω: "to mark off, hence metaph. to make separations: Ju 19."

[^c10-4]: The TR (Scrivener) reads οἱ ἀποδιορίζοντες ἑαυτούς. The Text-Critical Greek NT notes give the reading with ἑαυτούς in about 21% of manuscripts. Clement of Alexandria paraphrased: "Those, he says, separating the faithful from the unfaithful" (*Adumbrationes* on Jude 19, ANF 2).

[^c10-5]: Abbott-Smith, s.v. ψυχικός: "of the ψυχή (as the lower part of the immaterial in man), EV, natural: opp. to πνευματικός, I Co 2:14 15:44, 46; πνεῦμα μὴ ἔχοντες … Ju 19."


# Chapter 11. Building, Praying, Keeping, Waiting (Jude 20–21)

## A. The Text

> **20** But you, beloved, building yourselves up on your most holy faith, praying in the Holy Spirit, **21** keep yourselves in the love of God, waiting for the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ [resulting] in continuous life;
>
> — Jude 20–21, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

After the long exposure of the intruders, Jude finally tells his readers what to do. He began by urging them to "fight for the faith" (v. 3). Here is what that fight consists of. It is not, first of all, an attack on the intruders. It is a way of life:

- **building** one another up on the foundation of the holy faith;
- **praying** in the power of the Holy Spirit;
- **keeping** themselves in God's love;
- **waiting** with hope for the mercy of Jesus Christ that leads to eternal life.

The grammar shows what matters most. There is one main command, "keep yourselves in the love of God," and three supporting activities expressed as participles: building, praying, and waiting. These are the means by which believers remain in the love that God has already shown them (v. 1).

The passage also has a striking shape. It mentions the Holy Spirit, God (the Father), and the Lord Jesus Christ, and it gathers faith, love, and hope, the three great Christian virtues. In two verses Jude sketches the whole Christian life.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "But you, beloved"

For the third and last time, Jude addresses his readers as "beloved" (vv. 3, 17, 20). The emphatic Ὑμεῖς δέ (*Hymeis de*), "But you," repeats the turn of v. 17 and sets the readers against the intruders just described. The intruders are "not having the Spirit" (v. 19). The readers pray "in the Holy Spirit" (v. 20). The contrast could hardly be sharper.

### "building yourselves up on your most holy faith"

The participle ἐποικοδομοῦντες (*epoikodomountes*), "building up on," comes from the language of construction: οἰκοδομέω (*oikodomeō*), "to build a house," with the prefix ἐπί (*epi*), "upon." The New Testament uses this image often for the growth of believers and of the church. Paul writes: "I have laid a foundation, and another builds on [it], for no one is able to lay another foundation except that which is laid, which is Jesus the Christ" (1 Cor 3:10–11, LSV). Believers are "built on the foundation of the apostles and prophets, Jesus Christ Himself being chief corner-[stone]" (Eph 2:20, LSV), and are to be "rooted and built up in Him, and confirmed in the faith" (Col 2:7, LSV).

**"Yourselves"** (ἑαυτούς, *heautous*) is a reflexive pronoun. In the plural it often has a reciprocal sense, "one another." Paul uses the same pronoun when he tells the Colossians to be "teaching and admonishing each other" (νουθετοῦντες ἑαυτούς, Col 3:16, LSV), and the Ephesians to be "forgiving one another" (χαριζόμενοι ἑαυτοῖς, Eph 4:32, LSV).[^c11-1] Jude is not describing private self-improvement. He is describing a community building each other up: "build up one another, as also you do" (1 Thess 5:11, LSV).

**"On your most holy faith"** (τῇ ἁγιωτάτῃ ὑμῶν πίστει, *tē hagiōtatē hymōn pistei*). The foundation is "the faith," the same faith "once delivered to the holy ones" that Jude urged his readers to contend for in v. 3. The phrase "your most holy faith" ties the end of the letter to its beginning. Contending for the faith (v. 3) and building on the faith (v. 20) are the same activity seen from two sides.

The adjective ἁγιωτάτῃ (*hagiōtatē*), "most holy," is the superlative of ἅγιος (*hagios*), "holy." It is the only superlative of that adjective in the New Testament.[^c11-2] Jude chooses the strongest possible word. The faith is not a human tradition to be adjusted to taste. It is "most holy," set apart by God and belonging to him. The intruders have turned grace into licentiousness (v. 4). The readers build on a faith that is holy through and through.

The Byzantine text puts the words in a different order, "on your most holy faith building yourselves up," but the meaning is the same.[^c11-3]

### "praying in the Holy Spirit"

The second participle, προσευχόμενοι (*proseuchomenoi*), "praying," is qualified by ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ (*en pneumati hagiō*), "in the Holy Spirit." See the deep dive below. In brief, it describes prayer that the Holy Spirit inspires, empowers, and guides. That is the opposite of the intruders, who are "not having the Spirit" (v. 19), and of their "dreaming" (v. 8), which Jude regards as false spirituality.

### "keep yourselves in the love of God"

Here is the main verb of the sentence and the central command of the letter: ἑαυτοὺς ἐν ἀγάπῃ θεοῦ τηρήσατε (*heautous en agapē theou tērēsate*), "keep yourselves in the love of God." The aorist imperative τηρήσατε (*tērēsate*) is the fifth and final use of τηρέω, "keep," the word that runs through the whole letter:

- the readers "having been kept in Jesus Christ" (v. 1);
- the angels who "did not keep" their place (v. 6);
- the angels whom God "has kept" in bonds (v. 6);
- the darkness that "has been kept" for the wandering stars (v. 13);
- and now, "keep yourselves" (v. 21).

The readers have been kept by God, and now they are to keep themselves. The angels did not keep their position. The readers must keep theirs. See the deep dive below.

**"The love of God"** (ἀγάπῃ θεοῦ, *agapē theou*) could grammatically mean either God's love for us or our love for God. In Jude, the first is clearly primary. The readers are "having been loved in God [the] Father" (v. 1). To "keep yourselves in the love of God" is to remain in the place where God's love has put you. Jesus said: "According as the Father loved Me, I also loved you, remain in My love; if you may keep My commands, you will remain in My love" (John 15:9–10, LSV). Responding love for God is implied, but the foundation is God's love.

### "waiting for the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ"

The third participle is προσδεχόμενοι (*prosdechomenoi*), "waiting for, expecting." It is the word used of Simeon, who was "looking for the comforting of Israel" (Luke 2:25, LSV), and of believers "waiting for the blessed hope and appearing of the glory of our great God and Savior Jesus Christ" (Titus 2:13, LSV). It describes active, eager expectation, not passive delay.

What the readers wait for is "the mercy (ἔλεος, *eleos*) of our Lord Jesus Christ." Mercy was the first gift Jude wished his readers (v. 2). Here it is the mercy they will receive at Christ's coming, when he comes to judge (vv. 14–15). Paul prays for a friend, "may the LORD give to him to find kindness from the LORD in that day" (2 Tim 1:18, LSV), using the same Greek word, ἔλεος. The coming that means judgment for the ungodly (vv. 14–15) means mercy for those who wait for it.

### "[resulting] in continuous life"

The final phrase, εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον (*eis zōēn aiōnion*), "into eternal life," names the goal of the waiting. The LSV supplies "[resulting]" to show that the Greek preposition εἰς expresses direction or result. Christ's mercy leads to eternal life. The LSV's "continuous life" reflects its consistent rendering of αἰώνιος (*aiōnios*). In New Testament usage the phrase means both life without end and the life that belongs to the age to come.

The contrast with the intruders' destiny is deliberate. For them, "the gloom of the darkness throughout the age has been kept" (v. 13) and "continuous fire" is the example (v. 7). For the readers, the same word αἰώνιος describes life. Two destinies are set before the readers, and Jude's command is to remain in the love that leads to life.

### The shape of vv. 20–21

The sentence has a clear structure:

| Element | Greek | Relation |
|---|---|---|
| building yourselves up on your most holy faith | ἐποικοδομοῦντες | participle (means) |
| praying in the Holy Spirit | προσευχόμενοι | participle (means) |
| **keep yourselves in the love of God** | **τηρήσατε** | **main imperative** |
| waiting for the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ | προσδεχόμενοι | participle (accompanying attitude) |
| into eternal life | εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον | goal |

Two further patterns appear:

- **A Trinitarian pattern:** the Holy Spirit (v. 20), the love of God the Father (v. 21), and the mercy of the Lord Jesus Christ (v. 21). Jude does not offer a doctrine of the Trinity, but he speaks naturally of Spirit, Father, and Son as together the source of the Christian's life. Paul does the same in 2 Cor 13:14.
- **A pattern of faith, love, and hope:** "your most holy faith" (v. 20), "the love of God" (v. 21), and "waiting" for mercy and eternal life (v. 21), which is hope. Paul's famous triad is "faith, hope, love—these three" (1 Cor 13:13, LSV). He also speaks of "your work of faith, and the labor of the love, and the endurance of the hope" (1 Thess 1:3, LSV). Jude's version may reflect a shared early Christian pattern.

### Deep dive: Praying in the Holy Spirit

What does it mean to pray "in the Holy Spirit"? The phrase ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ can be read in several ways, and Christian traditions have emphasized different aspects.

**The New Testament background.** The closest parallel is Eph 6:18: "praying at all times in the Spirit, and in this, watching in all perseverance and supplication for all the holy ones" (LSV). Paul also describes the Spirit's role in prayer:

- The Spirit enables the most basic Christian prayer, the cry to God as Father: "you received [the] Spirit of adoption in which we cry, 'Abba! Father!'" (Rom 8:15, LSV); "God sent forth the Spirit of His Son into your hearts, crying, 'Abba! Father!'" (Gal 4:6, LSV).
- The Spirit helps believers in their weakness: "the Spirit helps our weaknesses; for what we may pray for, as it is necessary, we have not known, but [this] One—the Spirit—makes intercession for us with unutterable groanings" (Rom 8:26, LSV).
- In 1 Corinthians, Paul speaks of praying "in an [unknown] tongue" and says, "I will pray [in] the spirit, and I will also pray [with] understanding" (1 Cor 14:14–15, LSV).

Jesus speaks of worship "in spirit and truth" (John 4:23–24), and Paul of "serving God by the Spirit" (Phil 3:3, LSV).

**Interpretations.**

1. **Prayer inspired and guided by the Spirit.** Most interpreters understand Jude to mean prayer that is prompted, empowered, and shaped by the Holy Spirit, prayer that flows from the Spirit's presence in the believer and the community. On this reading, the phrase describes all genuine Christian prayer, including the "Abba, Father" of adoption and the Spirit's help in weakness.
2. **Charismatic or ecstatic prayer.** Some interpreters, especially in Pentecostal and charismatic traditions, see in Jude 20 a reference to Spirit-inspired prayer of the kind described in 1 Corinthians 14, including prayer in tongues. That is a possible reading, since Jude's communities, like Paul's, were probably familiar with such phenomena. Nothing in Jude's wording, however, requires it.
3. **Prayer within the sphere of the Spirit**, as opposed to the "natural" sphere of v. 19. This reading emphasizes the contrast with the intruders. Whatever the intruders' spiritual claims, true prayer happens in the realm of the Holy Spirit.

These readings are not exclusive. What is clear from the context is Jude's contrast. The intruders appealed to "dreams" and claimed spiritual superiority, but in Jude's judgment they were "not having the Spirit." The readers' answer is not to reject spiritual experience but to pray in the Spirit, inside the community, on the foundation of the faith, and in the love of God. In Jude's view, genuine spirituality is marked by prayer, holiness, love, and humility, not by private revelations that license sin.

**Prayer as part of contending.** It is significant that prayer is one of the means by which believers "fight for the faith" (v. 3). Jude does not imagine that false teaching can be met only with arguments. A praying community, dependent on the Spirit, is itself a defense of the faith.

### Deep dive: Keeping oneself in the love of God

Jude's central command, "keep yourselves in the love of God," raises a question that runs through the whole letter: how does the believer's keeping relate to God's keeping?

**God's keeping comes first.** The letter opens by describing the readers as "having been kept in Jesus Christ" (v. 1), or, as the Greek dative may also mean, kept *for* him. The perfect participle describes a settled state. It closes with praise to "Him who is able to guard you without stumbling" (v. 24). The readers' security rests on God's action, which frames the whole letter.

**The believer's keeping is real.** Between those two statements stands the command of v. 21: "keep yourselves." It is not a suggestion. The negative examples in the letter show what failure to keep looks like: the wilderness generation that did not believe (v. 5) and the angels who "did not keep their own principality" (v. 6).

**How the two fit together.** Jude does not explain the relationship theoretically, but the structure of vv. 20–21 suggests a practical answer. The believer keeps himself or herself in God's love not by heroic effort but by staying where God's love is found: building on the faith, praying in the Spirit, and waiting for Christ's mercy. These are not ways of earning God's love. They are ways of remaining in it. Jesus' words in John 15 offer a parallel: "remain in My love; if you may keep My commands, you will remain in My love" (John 15:9–10, LSV). The love comes first, and obedience is the way of abiding in it.

An image may help. The love of God is like a sheltered harbor. God has brought the readers into it (v. 1), and God is able to keep them there (v. 24). The command "keep yourselves in the love of God" means: do not sail out of the harbor. Build up the community, pray, wait for the Lord, and you will remain where you have been placed.

**Different traditions, one command.** As noted in Chapter 1, Christian traditions explain the relationship between divine preservation and human perseverance differently. All can agree on Jude's practical point: the command to keep oneself in God's love is meant seriously, and the means of obeying it are building, praying, and waiting.

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ἐποικοδομέω | *epoikodomeō* | build upon | "building yourselves up" | Construction image (1 Cor 3:10–14; Eph 2:20; Col 2:7). |
| ἑαυτούς | *heautous* | yourselves; one another | "yourselves" | Plural reflexive can be reciprocal (Col 3:16; Eph 4:32). |
| ἁγιώτατος | *hagiōtatos* | most holy | "most holy" | Only superlative of ἅγιος in the NT. |
| πίστις | *pistis* | faith | "faith" | The faith of v. 3. |
| προσεύχομαι | *proseuchomai* | pray | "praying" | |
| ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ | *en pneumati hagiō* | in the Holy Spirit | "in the Holy Spirit" | See deep dive. |
| τηρέω | *tēreō* | keep | "keep" | Aorist imperative; fifth use. |
| ἀγάπη θεοῦ | *agapē theou* | love of God | "the love of God" | Primarily God's love for believers. |
| προσδέχομαι | *prosdechomai* | wait for, expect | "waiting for" | Eager expectation (Luke 2:25; Titus 2:13). |
| ἔλεος | *eleos* | mercy | "the mercy" | Echoes v. 2. |
| ζωὴ αἰώνιος | *zōē aiōnios* | eternal life | "continuous life" | Life of the age to come. |

**A grammatical note on the participles.** Greek participles that accompany an imperative often take on some of its force. Many translations therefore render vv. 20–21 as a series of commands: "build yourselves up … pray … keep yourselves … wait" (compare the WEB and BSB). That is legitimate. It is still worth seeing that Jude subordinates building, praying, and waiting to the single command to keep oneself in God's love. They are not three extra tasks. They are how the one task is done.

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Prayer in early Christian gatherings.** From the beginning, prayer was central to Christian community life. The Jerusalem believers were "continuing steadfastly in … the prayers" (Acts 2:42, LSV). Paul's letters are full of prayer reports and requests. The *Didache* provides texts of thanksgiving prayers and instructs believers to pray the Lord's Prayer three times a day.[^c11-4] Jude's call to pray in the Spirit belongs to this common practice. Since the love feasts were also occasions of prayer, the instruction may have had a communal setting in mind.

**Waiting for the Lord.** The early Christians lived in expectation of Christ's return. The Aramaic prayer *Maranatha*, usually understood as ‘Our Lord, come!’ (1 Cor 16:22; *Didache* 10.6), preserved this hope in the language of the earliest Palestinian believers. (The LSV renders it "The LORD has come!" in 1 Cor 16:22, reflecting a different way of dividing the Aramaic words.)[^c11-5] Jude's "waiting for the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ" expresses the same expectation, colored by the judgment scene he has just quoted.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**1 Corinthians 3:10–11; Ephesians 2:20–22; Colossians 2:7; Acts 20:32 (building imagery).** The church as a building on the foundation of Christ and the apostolic faith. Jude's "building yourselves up on your most holy faith" belongs to this family of images.

**Ephesians 6:18; Romans 8:15–16, 26–27; Galatians 4:6 (prayer in the Spirit).** The closest parallels to Jude's "praying in the Holy Spirit."

**John 15:9–10; 1 John 4:16 (remaining in love).** "God is love, and he who is remaining in love, remains in God, and God in him" (1 John 4:16, LSV). The Johannine language of "remaining" in God's love parallels Jude's "keep yourselves in the love of God."

**Romans 5:5; 8:39 (the love of God).** "The love of God has been poured forth in our hearts through the Holy Spirit" (Rom 5:5, LSV). Paul, like Jude, connects God's love and the Spirit.

**Titus 2:13; Luke 2:25 (waiting).** The same verb of eager expectation.

**1 Corinthians 13:13; 1 Thessalonians 1:3; 5:8 (faith, love, hope).** The triad that Jude's vv. 20–21 seem to echo.

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. Is the reference to the Holy Spirit Trinitarian?** Jude's mention of the Holy Spirit, God, and the Lord Jesus Christ in two verses is often noted as an early example of the threefold pattern that later Christian theology articulated as the doctrine of the Trinity. Jude does not reflect on the relationship among the three. His language reflects the experience of early Christians, who knew God as Father, Jesus as Lord, and the Spirit as present power.

**2. What kind of prayer is "in the Holy Spirit"?** See the deep dive. Interpretations range from all Spirit-led prayer to specifically charismatic forms.

**3. Is "the love of God" God's love or our love?** God's love is primary (v. 1), though a responding love is implied.

**4. Is the "mercy" of v. 21 present or future?** It is future, the mercy shown at Christ's coming and judgment. The waiting has present effects: it shapes how believers live now.

## H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 20–21 are the positive counterpart to the whole central section. Each element answers something in the description of the intruders:

| The intruders | The readers |
|---|---|
| pervert grace into license (v. 4) | build on the most holy faith (v. 20) |
| "dreaming" (v. 8); "not having the Spirit" (v. 19) | praying in the Holy Spirit (v. 20) |
| stains (or reefs) at the love feasts (v. 12) | keep themselves in the love of God (v. 21) |
| darkness kept for them forever (v. 13) | waiting for mercy leading to eternal life (v. 21) |

The passage also completes the frame that began in vv. 1–2. Love, keeping, mercy, God, and Jesus Christ all return. Verse 3's call to contend for the faith is now explained: contending means building, praying, keeping, and waiting.

Finally, vv. 20–21 prepare for vv. 22–23. Those who are kept in God's love and wait for Christ's mercy are the ones who can show mercy to others. The movement is from receiving mercy to extending it.

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**The positive shape of faithfulness.** When Christians face false teaching or moral confusion, it is easy to define faithfulness only by what one opposes. Jude's instructions are almost entirely positive: build, pray, keep, wait. Communities under pressure may find that their best defense is a deep, positive spiritual life rather than constant vigilance against enemies.

**Community, not isolation.** "Building yourselves up" is communal. Jude assumes that believers grow together, through shared teaching, worship, encouragement, and accountability. Modern individualism can make faith a private project. Jude's grammar resists that.

**Prayer as spiritual defense.** Prayer in the Spirit is part of contending for the faith. Churches facing division or confusion might give prayer, honest and dependent, a central place in their response, not merely a ceremonial one.

**Living from love toward hope.** The passage moves from the love of God, already given, to the mercy of Christ, still awaited. The Christian life is lived between those two points, secure in love and leaning toward hope. That orientation may help believers handle both complacency and anxiety.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. Jude's response to false teaching is mostly positive: build, pray, keep, wait. Why might that be more effective than a purely defensive strategy?
2. How do you understand "praying in the Holy Spirit"? How has your tradition shaped your understanding?
3. What practices help you, or your community, "keep" yourselves in the love of God? Which of Jude's three activities (building, praying, waiting) is most neglected in your experience?
4. Jude speaks of the faith as "most holy." What does it mean to treat the faith as holy rather than as a human tradition to be adjusted?
5. How does waiting for "the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ" shape the way you think about the future and live in the present?

## K. Sources

- LSV texts: eBible.org edition. Greek: SBLGNT and apparatus (RP word order). Greek of Col 3:16 and Eph 4:32 from SBLGNT/MorphGNT.
- Superlative count: MorphGNT/SBLGNT.
- *Didache* 8; 10, ANF 7.
- Abbott-Smith, s.vv. τηρέω, ἔλεος.

[^c11-1]: SBLGNT Col 3:16: διδάσκοντες καὶ νουθετοῦντες ἑαυτούς; Eph 4:32: χαριζόμενοι ἑαυτοῖς. The LSV renders these "teaching and admonishing each other" and "forgiving one another."

[^c11-2]: Computed from MorphGNT/SBLGNT: the only superlative form of ἅγιος in the New Testament is ἁγιωτάτῃ in Jude 20.

[^c11-3]: SBLGNT apparatus: "ἐποικοδομοῦντες ἑαυτοὺς τῇ ἁγιωτάτῃ ὑμῶν πίστει WH Treg NIV ] τῇ ἁγιωτάτῃ ὑμῶν πίστει ἐποικοδομοῦντες ἑαυτοὺς RP."

[^c11-4]: *Didache* 8.2–3 (the Lord's Prayer, "Thrice in the day thus pray") and 9–10 (thanksgiving prayers), ANF 7 (<https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0714.htm>). The citation of chapter 8 is from the same New Advent text consulted for chapters 9–12.

[^c11-5]: *Didache* 10.6 in the original Greek reads μαραναθά. The New Advent (ANF) translation renders the passage; the Aramaic term itself is also found at 1 Cor 16:22.


# Chapter 12. Mercy for the Wavering, Rescue from the Fire (Jude 22–23)

## A. The Text

> **22** and, indeed, show mercy to those who are doubting, **23** and rescue others by snatching [them] out of fire; but show mercy to others in fear, hating even the coat having been stained from the flesh.
>
> — Jude 22–23, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

Jude's last instructions turn from the readers' own spiritual life (vv. 20–21) to their responsibility toward others. Those who have been kept in God's love and who wait for Christ's mercy must now show mercy themselves. Jude names three kinds of people (on the most widely accepted reading of the text):

1. **Those who are wavering**, unsettled by the intruders' influence. Show them mercy.
2. **Those in immediate danger**, already near the fire of judgment. Snatch them out.
3. **Others who are deeply compromised.** Show them mercy too, but with fear, hating even the clothing stained by their sin.

These two verses transform the letter. After so much warning and condemnation, Jude's last practical word is mercy. The readers are not to write off everyone touched by the intruders. They are to distinguish between people in different situations and to act toward each with compassion, urgency, and wisdom. Contending for the faith (v. 3) ends in rescue.

The Greek text of these verses is one of the most complicated in the New Testament. The manuscripts preserve two-clause and three-clause versions with different verbs. This chapter follows the three-clause text printed in the LSV, the SBLGNT, and NA28, and explains the alternatives in a deep dive.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "and, indeed, show mercy"

The command ἐλεᾶτε (*eleate*), "show mercy, have mercy," is a present imperative of ἐλεάω (*eleaō*), a variant form of the more common ἐλεέω (*eleeō*). The Byzantine manuscripts read ἐλεεῖτε, the more familiar form.[^c12-1] The word picks up the theme of mercy that frames the letter. Jude wished his readers mercy (v. 2), and they wait for Christ's mercy (v. 21). Now they are to show mercy. Mercy received becomes mercy given.

The construction καὶ οὓς μὲν … οὓς δὲ … οὓς δὲ (*kai hous men … hous de … hous de*), "and some … others … others," divides the people in view into groups. The LSV's "and, indeed, … others … others" reflects it.

### "to those who are doubting"

The participle διακρινομένους (*diakrinomenous*) comes from διακρίνω (*diakrinō*), a verb with a wide range: "to separate, distinguish, judge," and in the middle voice "to dispute" or "to doubt, waver." Jude used the same verb in v. 9 for Michael "disputing" with the Devil. Here two meanings are possible:

1. **"Those who doubt" or "waver."** This is the sense the verb usually has in the New Testament when it describes a person's inner state. Jesus says, "if you may have faith, and may not doubt" (Matt 21:21, LSV). James writes, "let him ask in faith, doubting nothing, for he who is doubting has been like a wave of the sea, driven by wind and tossed" (Jas 1:6, LSV). On this reading, Jude refers to believers who have been unsettled by the intruders and are uncertain whom to believe. They need mercy, patient and compassionate help, not condemnation.
2. **"Those who dispute."** This sense matches Jude's own use in v. 9. It would refer to people who argue, perhaps those who have taken the intruders' side. Some manuscripts read "convict" or "refute" (ἐλέγχετε, *elenchete*) instead of "show mercy" in this clause, which would give "refute those who dispute."

Most translations and commentators prefer "doubt" or "waver." The image in James of the doubter as "a wave of the sea" offers an interesting echo of Jude's "wild waves" (v. 13). The intruders are wild waves, and those they unsettle are tossed by them. The wavering are not the enemy. They are the ones at risk.

The Byzantine text reads the nominative διακρινόμενοι (*diakrinomenoi*), which makes the participle describe the readers rather than the people they help. The King James Version translates it "And of some have compassion, making a difference," meaning that the readers should show compassion while discerning between cases. That reading also has a good sense, but the accusative form, referring to those who doubt, has stronger early support and is printed in the critical editions.[^c12-2]

### "and rescue others by snatching [them] out of fire"

The second group is in more urgent danger. The command σῴζετε (*sōzete*), "save, rescue," is accompanied by the participle ἁρπάζοντες (*harpazontes*), "snatching, seizing." The verb ἁρπάζω (*harpazō*) means "to seize by force, to grab." Jesus uses it when he says that "no one will snatch them out of My hand" (John 10:28, LSV). Here it describes a rescuer pulling someone out of a burning building, an act of urgent, physical intervention.

The "fire" (πῦρ, *pyr*) recalls the "continuous fire" of Sodom (v. 7), the fire of judgment. These people are already, so to speak, in the flames, close to the fate of the intruders. They cannot be helped by gentle persuasion alone. They need decisive action.

The image comes from the prophets. In Zechariah's vision, the LORD says of Joshua the high priest: "Is this not a brand delivered from fire?" (Zech 3:2, LSV), a burning stick pulled out of the fire before it is consumed. The Septuagint's wording, δαλὸς ἐξεσπασμένος ἐκ πυρός (*dalos exespasmenos ek pyros*), "a brand snatched out of fire," is close to Jude's ἐκ πυρὸς ἁρπάζοντες (*ek pyros harpazontes*).[^c12-3] Amos uses the same image in a passage that also recalls Sodom and Gomorrah: "I have overturned among you, / Like the overturn by God of Sodom and Gomorrah, / And you are as a brand delivered from a burning" (Amos 4:11, LSV). Jude's readers, who have just heard about Sodom's fire (v. 7), are called to be the hands that pull people from it.

Notice where this allusion comes from. Zechariah 3 also stood behind Michael's words in v. 9: "The LORD rebuke you!" In that chapter, the accuser stands against Joshua, the LORD rebukes the accuser, Joshua is called "a brand delivered from fire," and his filthy garments are removed (Zech 3:1–4). Jude draws on the same scene twice. In v. 9 it shows the restraint of the heavenly defender. In v. 23 it shows the rescue of the one accused.

### "but show mercy to others in fear"

The third group also receives mercy, ἐλεᾶτε again, but now ἐν φόβῳ (*en phobō*), "in fear" or "with fear." What kind of fear?

1. **Fear of God.** Mercy must be shown with reverent awareness of God's holiness and judgment. The readers must not treat sin lightly while showing mercy to sinners. Paul writes, "having known, therefore, the fear of the LORD, we persuade men" (2 Cor 5:11, LSV), and Hebrews calls believers to serve God "with reverence and fear, for our God [is] also a consuming fire" (Heb 12:28–29, LSV).
2. **Caution about contamination.** Those who help people deeply involved in sin must take care not to be drawn into it themselves. Paul gives the same caution: "you who [are] spiritual restore such a one in a spirit of meekness, considering yourself—lest you also may be tempted" (Gal 6:1, LSV).

The two senses go together, and the next phrase explains the second.

### "hating even the coat having been stained from the flesh"

The final phrase is vivid: μισοῦντες καὶ τὸν ἀπὸ τῆς σαρκὸς ἐσπιλωμένον χιτῶνα (*misountes kai ton apo tēs sarkos espilōmenon chitōna*), "hating even the tunic stained by the flesh."

- The **χιτών** (*chitōn*) was the basic garment of the ancient world, a tunic worn next to the skin under the outer cloak.
- **ἐσπιλωμένον** (*espilōmenon*), "stained, spotted," is the perfect passive participle of σπιλόω (*spiloō*), "to stain, defile."[^c12-4] It belongs to the same family as σπίλος, "stain," and, on one reading, as the σπιλάδες of v. 12. If Jude intended the link, the intruders who were "stains" at the love feasts (LSV) have stained the garments of those they influenced.
- **"From the flesh"** (ἀπὸ τῆς σαρκός, *apo tēs sarkos*) points to the source of the stain: sinful human nature, and especially the sexual sins Jude has repeatedly mentioned (vv. 4, 7–8).

The image is deliberately strong. The undergarment touched the body directly. To hate "even the coat" is to want nothing to do with the sin, not even with what has been in contact with it, while still reaching out to the person. Again Zechariah 3 is in the background: "Joshua was clothed with filthy garments," and the LORD says, "Turn aside the filthy garments from off him. … See, I have caused your iniquity to pass away from off you, so as to clothe you with costly apparel" (Zech 3:3–4, LSV). The garment is not the person. The garment can be removed, and the person can be clothed afresh.

The phrase holds together two attitudes that are easily separated: mercy toward people and hatred of sin. Christians have often summarized the balance in the slogan "love the sinner, hate the sin." Jude gives a more demanding picture. The mercy is real, but it is shown "in fear," and the hatred of sin is strong enough to recoil from its slightest trace.

### Deep dive: Showing mercy to those who doubt

Jude's instruction to "show mercy to those who are doubting" is one of the most pastorally significant sentences in the letter, and it deserves reflection.

**Who are the doubters?** In Jude's situation, they were probably believers who had been exposed to the intruders' teaching and conduct and had become unsure. Perhaps the intruders' visions impressed them, or their freedom seemed attractive. Perhaps they could not see why Jude and the other leaders objected. Such people were not yet committed to the intruders' way of life, but they were wavering.

**Why mercy?** Jude could have told his readers to rebuke or exclude the doubters. Instead he tells them to show mercy. Several reasons emerge from the letter:

- **They have received mercy themselves** (vv. 2, 21). Those who wait for Christ's mercy cannot withhold mercy from others.
- **Doubt is not the same as rebellion.** The intruders deliberately turn grace into license and reject authority. The doubters are uncertain. To treat uncertainty as rebellion would push the wavering toward the very people Jude warns against.
- **The goal is restoration.** Jude's whole letter aims to keep people in God's love. Mercy toward the wavering serves that goal.

**What does mercy look like?** Jude does not spell it out, but the New Testament offers many models. Paul urges, "receive him who is weak in the faith—not to determinations of reasonings" (Rom 14:1, LSV). The Lord's servant "must not quarrel, but to be gentle to all, apt to teach, patient under evil, instructing those opposing in meekness—if perhaps God may give to them conversion" (2 Tim 2:24–25, LSV). James promises that "he who turned back a sinner from the straying of his way will save a soul from death" (Jas 5:20, LSV). Jesus' parable of the shepherd who leaves the ninety-nine to seek the one gone astray ends, "it is not [the] will in [the] presence of your Father who is in the heavens that one of these little ones may perish" (Matt 18:14, LSV). Mercy to the doubting likely includes patience, gentle instruction, prayer, presence, and the steady testimony of a community that is building itself up in the faith (v. 20).

**Discernment.** Jude's threefold distinction shows that mercy is not uniform. The wavering need patient compassion. Those near the fire need urgent rescue. The deeply compromised need mercy mixed with caution. Wise pastoral care pays attention to the person's situation.

**Doubt today.** The word "doubt" covers many experiences: intellectual questions, emotional struggles, confusion caused by conflicting teachers, disappointment with the church. Not all of them are what Jude had in mind. His instruction, though, suggests a general principle. Communities that hold firmly to "the faith once delivered" should be the most merciful toward those who struggle to hold it. Firmness and mercy are not opposites in Jude. Both flow from being kept in God's love.

### Deep dive: The text of Jude 22–23

Few New Testament passages survive in as many forms as these two verses. The main forms can be grouped as follows (Greek simplified):[^c12-5]

| Witness | Text | Structure |
|---|---|---|
| SBLGNT, NA28, Tregelles, NIV Greek text; LSV | καὶ οὓς μὲν **ἐλεᾶτε** διακρινομένους, οὓς δὲ σῴζετε ἐκ πυρὸς ἁρπάζοντες, οὓς δὲ ἐλεᾶτε ἐν φόβῳ… | Three groups: show mercy to the doubting / save others, snatching from fire / show mercy to others in fear |
| Tregelles (first verb), Tyndale House GNT (as reported) | καὶ οὓς μὲν **ἐλέγχετε** διακρινομένους… | Three groups, but the first is "refute (or convict) those who dispute" |
| Westcott–Hort | καὶ οὓς μὲν ἐλεᾶτε διακρινομένους σῴζετε ἐκ πυρὸς ἁρπάζοντες, οὓς δὲ ἐλεᾶτε ἐν φόβῳ… | Two groups: "have mercy on some who doubt, saving them by snatching them from fire; and on others have mercy in fear" |
| Robinson–Pierpont, TR (KJV) | καὶ οὓς μὲν ἐλεεῖτε διακρινόμενοι, οὓς δὲ ἐν φόβῳ σῴζετε ἐκ (τοῦ) πυρὸς ἁρπάζοντες… | Two groups: "on some have compassion, making a difference; others save with fear, pulling them out of the fire" |
| P72 (early papyrus) | οὓς μὲν ἐκ πυρὸς ἁρπάσατε, διακρινομένους δὲ ἐλεεῖτε ἐν φόβῳ… | Two groups: "snatch some from the fire, but on those who doubt have mercy in fear" |

Early Christian writers knew different forms. Clement of Alexandria paraphrased a two-part text: "But some, he says, save, plucking them from the fire; but of some have compassion in fear."[^c12-6]

**Why so many forms?** The repetition of similar words (two forms of "show mercy," and the series οὓς μὲν … οὓς δέ) made the passage easy to miscopy. Scribes may have dropped a clause by accident when the eye skipped from one οὓς δέ to the next, or they may have smoothed a difficult text. The verb διακρίνω, which can mean "doubt" or "dispute," also invited different interpretations and therefore different verbs ("show mercy" or "refute").

**Which is original?** Scholars are divided. Many accept the three-clause text printed in the SBLGNT and NA28, which has good early support and explains the two-clause forms as accidental omissions. Others, including Richard Bauckham, have argued that a shorter two-clause form closer to P72 is original and that the three-clause text arose when scribes expanded it.[^c12-7]

**Does it matter?** The core of the passage is the same in all forms: mercy toward some, urgent rescue of others from the fire, and caution about the defilement of sin. The differences affect how many groups Jude distinguishes and whether the first group receives mercy or rebuke. This book follows the three-clause text of the LSV and SBLGNT, while recognizing that certainty is not possible.

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ἐλεάω / ἐλεέω | *eleaō* / *eleeō* | show mercy | "show mercy" | Present imperative; variant ἐλέγχετε, "convict." |
| διακρίνομαι | *diakrinomai* | dispute; doubt, waver | "those who are doubting" | Same verb as v. 9; Jas 1:6. |
| σῴζω | *sōzō* | save, rescue | "rescue" | |
| ἁρπάζω | *harpazō* | seize, snatch | "snatching" | Urgent action; John 10:28. |
| πῦρ | *pyr* | fire | "fire" | Judgment fire (v. 7); Zech 3:2; Amos 4:11. |
| φόβος | *phobos* | fear, reverence | "in fear" | Fear of God and caution. |
| μισέω | *miseō* | hate | "hating" | |
| χιτών | *chitōn* | tunic, undergarment | "the coat" | Garment worn next to the skin. |
| σπιλόω | *spiloō* | stain, defile | "stained" | Perfect passive participle; cf. σπιλάδες (v. 12). |
| σάρξ | *sarx* | flesh | "the flesh" | Sinful human nature; cf. vv. 7–8. |

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Clothing and purity.** In the Jewish world, garments could become ritually impure through contact with bodily discharges or with unclean things (Lev 13–15), and purification involved washing clothes. The image of a defiled garment would have carried both ritual and moral overtones. Zechariah's vision of the high priest's filthy garments, which are removed and replaced with clean ones, provided a powerful picture of forgiveness and restoration.

**Fire as judgment.** In Jewish and early Christian thought, fire was the most common image of divine judgment: the fire of Sodom, the fire of Gehenna, the "continuous fire" of the final judgment. To snatch someone from the fire was to rescue them from judgment, by persuasion, intervention, or prayer, before it was too late.

**Church discipline and restoration.** Early Christian communities practiced various forms of discipline toward members who fell into serious sin (Matt 18:15–17; 1 Cor 5; 2 Thess 3:14–15). The goal was always restoration: "do not count as an enemy, but admonish as a brother" (2 Thess 3:15, LSV). Jude's instructions belong to this pastoral tradition, though they do not describe formal procedures.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**Zechariah 3:1–5 (allusion).** The brand snatched from the fire and the filthy garments removed. This is the most important background for both images in v. 23, and the same chapter stands behind Michael's words in v. 9.

**Amos 4:11 (shared image).** "A brand delivered from a burning," in a passage that also mentions Sodom and Gomorrah.

**James 1:6; 5:19–20 (shared themes).** The doubter as a wave of the sea, and the saving of a sinner from death. James's closing verses resemble Jude's in spirit: both letters end by urging readers to restore those who wander.

**Galatians 6:1; 2 Timothy 2:24–26; Romans 14:1 (pastoral care).** Gentle restoration with awareness of one's own vulnerability.

**Matthew 18:12–14 (the lost sheep).** God's will that none of the little ones perish.

**Revelation 3:4; 2 Corinthians 7:1 (defiled garments; cleansing).** "a few names even in Sardis who did not defile their garments" (Rev 3:4, LSV); "may we cleanse ourselves from every defilement of flesh and spirit, perfecting sanctification in the fear of God" (2 Cor 7:1, LSV). Paul joins cleansing from defilement of the flesh with "the fear of God," as Jude joins mercy "in fear" with hatred of the garment "stained from the flesh."

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. Two groups or three?** See the deep dive.

**2. "Doubt" or "dispute"?** Most interpreters prefer "doubt" or "waver," following New Testament usage and the logic of showing mercy. The alternative ("dispute," with "refute" or "convict") has some manuscript support and matches Jude's use of the verb in v. 9.

**3. Who is in the fire?** Probably people who have gone further than the doubters, adopting the intruders' practices. Some interpreters think the intruders themselves might be included among those who could still be snatched from the fire. Jude's harsh descriptions in vv. 4–19 do not encourage that reading, but his structure leaves the door open in a way that is worth noting.

**4. What does "in fear" mean?** Fear of God, caution about contamination, or both.

**5. How do vv. 22–23 relate to the rest of the letter?** Some readers find the shift from condemnation to mercy abrupt. It is better understood as the goal of the letter. Jude exposes the intruders in order to protect the community and rescue those at risk. The warnings serve the mercy.

## H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 22–23 complete the exhortation that began in v. 20. The readers who keep themselves in God's love (v. 21) now extend that love to others. The letter's three great themes come together here:

- **Mercy:** wished for the readers (v. 2), awaited from Christ (v. 21), shown to others (vv. 22–23).
- **Fire and judgment:** the fire of Sodom (v. 7), the coming judgment (vv. 14–15), and the fire from which people must be snatched (v. 23).
- **Defilement:** the flesh defiled by the intruders (v. 8), the stains at the love feasts (v. 12), and the garment stained by the flesh (v. 23).

The passage also prepares for the doxology. The readers who rescue others from stumbling into the fire will themselves be kept "without stumbling" by the God who is able (v. 24).

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**Mercy as the end of contending.** Jude's letter shows that contending for the faith does not end in triumph over opponents but in mercy toward the wavering and rescue of the endangered. Christians who are passionate about truth might ask whether their passion leads to the same place.

**Differentiated care.** Jude distinguishes among people in different situations. Pastoral care today might likewise distinguish between honest questioners, people in crisis, and people deeply entangled in harmful patterns, responding to each with appropriate compassion, urgency, and wisdom.

**Mercy without naïveté.** Showing mercy "in fear" and "hating even the coat" suggests that mercy does not mean ignoring danger. Those who help others out of destructive situations, whether addiction, abuse, or spiritual manipulation, often need to set boundaries and protect themselves. That is not a failure of mercy but part of it.

**The garment can be changed.** Zechariah's vision, behind Jude's words, ends with new clothes. Those who have been stained can be cleansed. Christians who show mercy do so in the hope that God will "turn aside the filthy garments" and clothe people afresh.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. Why do you think Jude ends his practical instructions with mercy rather than with further warnings? How does this change your reading of the whole letter?
2. Think of people you know who are "wavering" in faith. What would it look like to show them mercy, in practical terms?
3. Jude speaks of "snatching" some from the fire. Are there situations in which urgent intervention is more loving than patient waiting? How can one tell?
4. What does it mean to show mercy "in fear"? How can believers help others without being drawn into the same patterns?
5. The garment imagery comes from Zechariah 3, where filthy garments are removed and replaced. How does this picture of cleansing shape the way you think about people who have fallen into sin?

## K. Sources

- LXX Zech 3:2 (Brenton's Greek Septuagint).
- Clement of Alexandria, *Adumbrationes* on Jude 22–23, ANF 2.
- Abbott-Smith, s.v. σπιλόω.
- Bauckham, *Jude, 2 Peter*, on vv. 22–23; Tommy Wasserman, *The Epistle of Jude: Its Text and Transmission* (2006); Metzger, *Textual Commentary*, on Jude 22–23 (all cited from prior knowledge).

Manuscript counts do not determine originality; the critical editions weigh the age and quality of witnesses.

The P72 reading is given from the standard critical literature and was not checked against a printed apparatus for this edition.
