# Chapter 13. To Him Who Is Able: Kept from Stumbling (Jude 24–25)

## A. The Text

> **24** And to Him who is able to guard you without stumbling, and to set [you] in the presence of His glory unblemished, in gladness, **25** to the only wise God our Savior, [is] glory and greatness, power and authority, both now and forever! Amen.
>
> — Jude 24–25, LSV

## B. Plain-English Orientation

Jude ends his letter not with greetings or travel plans but with praise. Having warned his readers of danger, urged them to keep themselves in God's love, and told them to rescue others, he turns their eyes to God. God is able to keep them from stumbling. God will bring them into his glorious presence, blameless and full of joy. To this God, the only God and Savior, belong glory, majesty, power, and authority, through Jesus Christ, before all time, now, and forever.

This doxology is one of the most beloved in Scripture and is often used to close Christian worship. In the context of Jude it is also the letter's final word on its central theme. The readers have been kept (v. 1) and must keep themselves (v. 21), and now they are assured that God is able to guard them. The danger of stumbling is real, but the one who holds them is stronger.

**A note on the text.** The LSV's wording of v. 25 follows the Byzantine tradition and the Textus Receptus: "to the only wise God our Savior … both now and forever." The SBLGNT and NA28 have a longer text: "to the only God our Savior, **through Jesus Christ our Lord**, [be] glory, majesty, power, and authority **before all time** and now and forever." This chapter discusses both, since the differences are theologically interesting.

## C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

### "And to Him who is able"

The doxology begins with a dative, Τῷ δὲ δυναμένῳ (*Tō de dynamenō*), "Now to the one who is able." Its main clause comes only at the end: "to Him … [is] glory." Paul's letters close in a similar way: "And to Him who is able to establish you" (Rom 16:25, LSV); "and to Him who is able to do exceedingly [and] abundantly above all things that we ask or think" (Eph 3:20, LSV).

The emphasis falls on God's **ability**. Throughout the letter Jude has described powers that threaten the readers: intruders who slip in unnoticed, persuasive dreamers, slanderers, reefs, wild waves. Now he names the power that is greater. Everything depends not on the readers' strength but on God's.

### "to guard you without stumbling"

The infinitive φυλάξαι (*phylaxai*), "to guard," comes from φυλάσσω (*phylassō*), a near synonym of τηρέω (*tēreō*), "keep," the word that has run through the whole letter. φυλάσσω carries the idea of a guard posted to protect, "implying custody and protection."[^c13-1] Jesus used it in his prayer: "I guarded those whom You have given to Me, and none of them were destroyed" (John 17:12, LSV).

**A textual note.** The SBLGNT, NA28, and TR read ὑμᾶς (*hymas*), "you," and the LSV follows them. The Robinson–Pierpont text, following most Byzantine manuscripts, reads αὐτούς (*autous*), "them," which shifts the focus slightly.[^c13-2]

**"Without stumbling"** translates ἀπταίστους (*aptaistous*), an adjective found nowhere else in the New Testament. It is formed from πταίω (*ptaiō*), "to stumble, trip," with the negative prefix ἀ-, and means "not stumbling, sure-footed."[^c13-3] It is used in the Greek Bible only once elsewhere, in 3 Maccabees, where God rescues the Jews of Egypt "unharmed" from destruction (ἀπταίστους, 3 Macc 6:39).[^c13-4] See the deep dive below.

### "and to set [you] in the presence of His glory unblemished, in gladness"

God's ability extends beyond protection along the way to the destination. He is able στῆσαι κατενώπιον τῆς δόξης αὐτοῦ (*stēsai katenōpion tēs doxēs autou*), "to make [you] stand before his glory." The verb ἵστημι (*histēmi*), "to set, make stand," suggests standing upright in the presence of a king or judge. The adverb κατενώπιον (*katenōpion*) means "before, in the presence of."[^c13-5] "His glory" is the radiant presence of God, the glory that Moses longed to see and that the prophets described. The scene is the final judgment, the same judgment described in vv. 14–15. For the ungodly it means conviction. For those kept by God it means standing before his glory.

They will stand **"unblemished"** (ἀμώμους, *amōmous*). The word was originally used of sacrificial animals "without blemish," and then morally of people who are "faultless."[^c13-6] Paul uses it of the goal of God's work: "to present you holy, and unblemished, and unblameable before Himself" (Col 1:22, LSV); "that He might present the Assembly to Himself in glory, having no spot or wrinkle … but that it may be holy and unblemished" (Eph 5:27, LSV). In Jude the word stands in deliberate contrast to the intruders, who were "stains" (v. 12) and whose garments were "stained" by the flesh (v. 23). The readers will be presented without stain.

And they will stand **"in gladness"** (ἐν ἀγαλλιάσει, *en agalliasei*). The noun means "exultation, exuberant joy."[^c13-7] It is the joy of celebration, of a festival or a wedding. Peter uses the related verb: "rejoice, that you may also rejoice in the revelation of His glory—exulting" (1 Pet 4:13, LSV). After so much darkness, Jude's letter ends with the picture of believers standing before God's glory, faultless and overflowing with joy.

### "to the only wise God our Savior"

The God who can do all this is "the only God our Savior." The LSV, following the TR and Byzantine text, reads "only **wise** God" (μόνῳ σοφῷ θεῷ, *monō sophō theō*). The critical texts lack "wise." Most likely a scribe added it under the influence of Paul's doxology, "to the only wise God, through Jesus Christ, to Him [be] glory for all ages" (Rom 16:27, LSV; cf. 1 Tim 1:17).[^c13-8] On either reading, the emphasis falls on "only." There is one God. Earlier, Jude called Jesus Christ "our only Master and Lord" (v. 4). The two "only"s stand side by side without contradiction. For Jude, confessing Jesus as the only Master does not compromise the confession of one God.

**"Our Savior"** (σωτῆρι ἡμῶν, *sōtēri hēmōn*) gives God the title σωτήρ, "Savior, deliverer." In the Greco-Roman world the title was commonly given to kings and emperors.[^c13-9] In the Bible it belongs to God, who saved Israel from Egypt (v. 5) and who saves now. The letter that began with "our common salvation" (v. 3) ends by naming God "our Savior."

### ‘through Jesus Christ our Lord’ (critical text)

The critical texts include the phrase διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν (*dia Iēsou Christou tou kyriou hēmōn*), "through Jesus Christ our Lord." The phrase is absent from the Byzantine text, the TR, and therefore the LSV. It is well attested in early manuscripts, and most scholars regard it as original.[^c13-10]

If original, it says something important. Glory is given to God *through* Jesus Christ. That may mean that God's glory is revealed and exercised through Christ, or that believers give glory to God through Christ, or both. Paul's doxology in Romans has the same pattern ("to the only wise God, through Jesus Christ"). So does Peter's: "so that in all things God may be glorified through Jesus Christ, to whom is the glory and the power through the ages of the ages" (1 Pet 4:11, LSV). The doxology, like the whole letter, holds God and Jesus Christ together.

### "[is] glory and greatness, power and authority"

Four attributes are ascribed to God:

- **Glory** (δόξα, *doxa*): God's radiant splendor and honor. This is the "glory" before which believers will stand (v. 24).
- **Greatness** or **majesty** (μεγαλωσύνη, *megalōsynē*): a word of the Greek Bible, used in Hebrews of God as "the Greatness in the highest" (Heb 1:3, LSV).[^c13-11]
- **Power** or **dominion** (κράτος, *kratos*): might and sovereign rule, common in New Testament doxologies (1 Tim 6:16; 1 Pet 4:11; 5:11; Rev 1:6).[^c13-12]
- **Authority** (ἐξουσία, *exousia*): the right to rule.

The list recalls David's prayer: "To You, O YHWH, [is] the greatness, and the might, and the beauty, and the victory, and the splendor … to You, O YHWH, [is] the kingdom" (1 Chr 29:11, LSV). The intruders "disregard lordship" (v. 8) and slander glorious beings (v. 8). The doxology declares where true glory, majesty, and authority lie.

**"Is" or "be"?** The Greek has no verb. Translators must supply one. The LSV supplies "[is]," making the doxology a declaration: glory *is* God's. Most translations supply "be," making it a wish or prayer: glory *be* to God. Both are grammatically possible. In 1 Pet 4:11 a verb is expressed ("to whom *is* the glory," ᾧ ἐστιν ἡ δόξα), which shows that the declarative sense was natural to early Christians. Perhaps the best understanding holds both: glory belongs to God, and the worshiping community gladly acknowledges it.

### ‘before all time, and now, and forever’ (critical text) / "both now and forever" (LSV)

The critical text has a magnificent triple expression of time: πρὸ παντὸς τοῦ αἰῶνος καὶ νῦν καὶ εἰς πάντας τοὺς αἰῶνας (*pro pantos tou aiōnos kai nyn kai eis pantas tous aiōnas*), "before all the age, and now, and into all the ages." God's glory reaches back before creation, fills the present, and extends without end into the future. The phrase "before all time" is absent from the Byzantine text and the LSV, which reads "both now and forever."[^c13-13]

The longer form is unusual in New Testament doxologies. It is the only one that explicitly includes the eternity *before* time. It fits the letter's sweep, which ranges from the ancient past (Enoch, the angels, the exodus), through the present (the intruders and the readers), to the future (the coming of the Lord, eternal life). The God who was glorious before all ages is the God who keeps his people now and will present them in glory forever.

### "Amen."

The final word, ἀμήν (*amēn*), is a Hebrew word meaning "truly, so be it." It was the congregation's response to prayer and praise in the synagogue. Early Christians kept it in its Hebrew form. When Jude's letter was read aloud in the community, the people would likely have answered "Amen," making his doxology their own.

### Deep dive: Being kept from stumbling

Jude's last promise, that God is able to guard his readers "without stumbling," gathers up the letter's central theme.

**Stumbling in the Bible.** The image of the foot that slips is common in Scripture, especially in the Psalms. "He does not permit your foot to be moved, / He who is preserving you does not slumber" (Ps 121:3, LSV). "You have delivered my soul from death, / Do You not [keep] my feet from falling?" (Ps 56:13, LSV). "You have delivered my soul from death, / My eyes from tears, my feet from overthrowing" (Ps 116:8, LSV). In Psalm 73 the writer confesses, "As nothing, my steps have slipped, / For I have been envious of the boastful" (Ps 73:2, LSV). His stumbling began with envy of the arrogant, the kind of people Jude has described.

**What stumbling means in Jude.** In the context of the letter, to stumble is to fall away from "the faith once delivered" (v. 3). It means following the intruders into their perversion of grace, being swept along by wild waves, leaving the place God has given as the angels did (v. 6), or failing to believe as the wilderness generation did (v. 5). The letter has shown how real that danger is.

**The guard who does not sleep.** Against this danger stands the God who is "able." The verb φυλάσσω, "guard," suggests a sentry who keeps watch through the night. The Psalmist's "He who is preserving you does not slumber" captures the image (Ps 121:3, LSV). God's guarding does not depend on the believer's alertness. It is God's own vigilance.

**The relationship with v. 21.** How does God's guarding relate to the command "keep yourselves in the love of God" (v. 21)? Jude places both side by side without tension. God's ability to guard is the ground of the believer's confidence. The believer's keeping is the way that confidence is lived. The doxology does not cancel the command. It makes it possible. A believer who builds up, prays, and waits (vv. 20–21) does so in the assurance that God is able to finish what he began.

**Not perfection, but preservation.** "Without stumbling" does not mean that believers will never sin or struggle. The New Testament is honest about believers' failures ("we all make many stumbles," Jas 3:2, LSV). The promise is that God will keep them from the stumbling that leads to ruin, from falling away from him. The goal is to "set [you] in the presence of His glory unblemished," and that blamelessness is God's gift, not the reader's achievement.

**A word for the wavering.** Jude has just told his readers to show mercy to "those who are doubting" (v. 22). The doxology is good news for them too. The strength that keeps believers from stumbling is not their own certainty but God's ability. Those who waver may find their footing not by trying harder to be sure but by trusting the one who is able.

## D. Original-Language Investigation

| Greek | Transliteration | Basic meaning | In context | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| δύναμαι | *dynamai* | be able | "Him who is able" | Doxology opening (Rom 16:25; Eph 3:20). |
| φυλάσσω | *phylassō* | guard, protect | "to guard" | Synonym of τηρέω; "custody and protection." |
| ἄπταιστος | *aptaistos* | not stumbling, sure-footed | "without stumbling" | NT hapax; LXX only 3 Macc 6:39. |
| ἵστημι | *histēmi* | set, make stand | "to set" | Standing before a king or judge. |
| κατενώπιον | *katenōpion* | before, in the presence of | "in the presence of" | Eph 1:4; Col 1:22. |
| δόξα | *doxa* | glory | "His glory" / "glory" | |
| ἄμωμος | *amōmos* | without blemish | "unblemished" | Sacrificial and moral. |
| ἀγαλλίασις | *agalliasis* | exultation | "gladness" | Exuberant joy. |
| μόνος | *monos* | only | "only" | Cf. v. 4. |
| σοφός | *sophos* | wise | "wise" | TR/Byzantine only; cf. Rom 16:27. |
| σωτήρ | *sōtēr* | savior | "our Savior" | Royal title in the Greco-Roman world; in the Bible, of God. |
| μεγαλωσύνη | *megalōsynē* | greatness, majesty | "greatness" | Heb 1:3; 8:1. |
| κράτος | *kratos* | power, dominion | "power" | Doxological term. |
| ἐξουσία | *exousia* | authority | "authority" | |
| αἰών | *aiōn* | age | "forever" | "Before all the age … into all the ages." |
| ἀμήν | *amēn* | truly, so be it | "Amen" | Hebrew liturgical response. |

## E. The Historical and Cultural World

**Doxologies in Jewish and Christian worship.** Jewish prayer was rich in blessings and doxologies ascribing glory to God, and the five books of the Psalter each close with one (Pss 41:13; 72:18–19; 89:52; 106:48; 150). Early Christians continued the practice, and doxologies often closed letters that would be read in worship (Rom 16:25–27; Phil 4:20; 1 Tim 1:17; 1 Pet 4:11; 5:11; 2 Pet 3:18). Jude's letter, read aloud in the community, would end in worship.

**"Savior" in the Roman world.** Hellenistic kings and Roman emperors were often hailed as "savior" (σωτήρ) and "benefactor" in inscriptions and official language.[^c13-14] When Christians called God "our Savior" and gave him "glory," "power," and "authority," they were making a claim that implicitly relativized every human ruler. Jude's doxology is not a political manifesto, but its language would not have been neutral in the first-century world.

**Standing in the presence of a king.** In the ancient Near East, to stand in the presence of a king was an honor reserved for those admitted to court. To be "set" before God's glory "unblemished" and "in gladness" is the image of acceptance at the royal court of heaven.

## F. Connections Across Scripture

**Romans 16:25–27; Ephesians 3:20–21 (form).** The closest parallels in form: "to Him who is able … to the only wise God, through Jesus Christ, to Him [be] glory" (Rom 16:25, 27, LSV). The parallel probably explains the addition of "wise" in some manuscripts of Jude.

**1 Timothy 1:17; 1 Peter 4:11; 2 Peter 3:18 (doxologies).** Other New Testament doxologies use similar vocabulary. Second Peter ends: "to Him [is] the glory both now, and to the day of the age! Amen" (2 Pet 3:18, LSV), a doxology to Christ that resembles Jude's.

**Psalms 121; 56:13; 116:8 (being kept from stumbling).** The Old Testament roots of Jude's promise.

**Colossians 1:22; Ephesians 1:4; 5:27; 1 Thessalonians 3:13 (presentation unblemished).** The goal of God's work is a people presented holy and blameless before him.

**1 Chronicles 29:10–13 (attributes).** David's blessing of God, ascribing greatness, power, glory, and kingdom to him.

**John 17:5; 2 Timothy 1:9 (before time).** "The glory that I had with You before the world was" (John 17:5, LSV); grace "given to us in Christ Jesus, before the times of the ages" (2 Tim 1:9, LSV). These texts share the critical text's vision of glory before time.

## G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

**1. Which text of v. 25 is original?** Most scholars accept the longer text of the critical editions, with "through Jesus Christ our Lord" and "before all time," and without "wise." The Byzantine form is shorter in two places and adds "wise," probably from Rom 16:27. Readers of the LSV should know that their translation reflects the Byzantine form here.

**2. Is the doxology addressed to God or to Christ?** It is addressed to "the only God our Savior," with glory ascribed "through Jesus Christ" (critical text). Some later doxologies are addressed directly to Christ (2 Pet 3:18; Rev 5:13). Jude's is addressed to God through Christ, and that reflects the pattern of early Christian worship.

**3. Does "able to guard you without stumbling" guarantee perseverance?** As in Chapters 1 and 11, Christian traditions answer differently. All agree that the doxology expresses confidence in God's power and faithfulness. Some read it as a guarantee that true believers will persevere. Others read it as a statement of God's ability that believers must still respond to. Jude's placement of the doxology after v. 21 suggests that he did not see the two as competing.

**4. "Is" or "be"?** The verbless Greek allows both. The LSV's "[is]" emphasizes that glory already belongs to God. The traditional "be" emphasizes the worshipers' acknowledgment.

## H. The Passage Within Jude

The doxology completes the letter's frame. The opening described the readers as "called, … loved … and … kept" (v. 1). The ending praises God who is "able to guard" them (v. 24). Between these two statements of God's keeping lies everything else: the intruders, the examples of judgment, the woes, the images of disorder, the call to build, pray, keep, wait, and show mercy.

Every major theme of the letter is gathered in these two verses:

- **Keeping and guarding:** "able to guard you" (v. 24), completing the τηρέω thread (vv. 1, 6, 13, 21).
- **Stumbling and falling:** the danger shown by the wilderness generation, the angels, and the intruders, and averted by God's power.
- **Blemish and purity:** "unblemished" (v. 24), in contrast to the stains (v. 12) and the stained garment (v. 23).
- **Glory:** the "glorious [ones]" slandered by the intruders (v. 8), and the true glory of God before which the readers will stand (vv. 24–25).
- **Joy:** "in gladness" (v. 24), the final mood of a letter that began with mercy, peace, and love.
- **Salvation:** "our common salvation" (v. 3) and "God our Savior" (v. 25).
- **Lordship:** "our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ" (v. 4) and ‘through Jesus Christ our Lord’ (v. 25, critical text).
- **Time:** ancient examples, present dangers, and future judgment, all embraced by the eternal glory of God, ‘before all time, and now, and forever.’

Jude wanted to write about "our common salvation" (v. 3) and was forced to write a warning instead. In the doxology he finally gets to write about salvation, and he does it in praise.

## I. Contemporary Understanding

*Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.*

**Ending in worship.** Jude ends a hard letter with praise. Christians facing conflict, confusion, or danger might learn from his example to return, finally, to the worship of God, not as an escape from difficulty but as the truest response to it.

**Confidence that is not self-confidence.** The doxology's emphasis on God's ability frees believers from depending on their own steadiness. That is good news for the wavering, the weary, and those who have stumbled before.

**Joy as the end.** The final picture is of believers standing before God's glory "in gladness." Christian faith, for all its seriousness about sin and judgment, aims at joy. Communities that have forgotten that may need Jude's ending as much as his warnings.

**Using the doxology.** Jude 24–25 has long been used as a benediction at the close of Christian services. Its use may be enriched by remembering its context. It is spoken to people who face real dangers, by someone who knows those dangers well, in confidence that God is greater.

## J. Reflection and Discussion

1. How does reading the doxology after the rest of Jude change its meaning for you, compared with hearing it as a stand-alone benediction?
2. What does "stumbling" look like in your life or community? How does the promise that God "is able to guard you" speak to that?
3. Jude pictures believers standing before God's glory "unblemished, in gladness." What does that picture suggest about the goal of the Christian life?
4. The critical text includes "before all time, and now, and forever." What difference does it make to think of God's glory as stretching back before creation?
5. Jude both commands believers to keep themselves (v. 21) and praises God for keeping them (v. 24). How do you hold these together in your own life?

# Afterword: Reading Jude Whole {.unnumbered}

We have walked through Jude phrase by phrase, stopping at reefs and clouds, angels and archangels, Enoch and the love feast. It is worth stepping back, as Jude's first hearers would have done when the reading ended, and hearing the letter once more as a single word.

Jude wrote to people he loved about something he loved, "our common salvation." What he found was a community in danger. People had slipped in who spoke impressively, claimed visions, sat at the common table, and taught that grace made holiness unnecessary. Jude did not respond with a treatise. He responded with memory. Remember the people saved from Egypt who did not believe. Remember the angels who left their place and Sodom's fire. Remember Michael, who did not presume to judge even the Devil. Remember Cain, Balaam, and Korah. Remember what Enoch said and what the apostles told you. In each memory, Jude saw the present more clearly. The story of God's people had seen this danger before, and God had not been defeated by it.

Yet the letter does not end in memory, and it certainly does not end in denunciation. It ends with a community building itself up on a most holy faith, praying in the Holy Spirit, keeping itself in the love of God, and waiting for the mercy of Jesus Christ. It ends with that community reaching out in mercy to the wavering, snatching the endangered from the fire, and showing compassion even where caution is needed. And it ends with praise to the God who is able to keep his people from stumbling and to bring them, faultless and rejoicing, into the presence of his glory.

Three sentences may summarize what Jude asks of every generation that reads him:

**Remember what God has done and said.** The faith was delivered once for all. Its shape is known. Novelty is not the measure of truth.

**Keep yourselves in the love of God.** Faithfulness is not mainly a matter of fighting enemies but of remaining where God's love has placed us, through building up one another, prayer, and hope.

**Show mercy.** Those who contend most earnestly for the faith should be the quickest to reach out to the doubting and the endangered, because they themselves live by mercy.

Around these three sentences stand the two great statements that frame the letter: you have been called, loved, and kept; and God is able to guard you without stumbling. Between them lies everything else, the danger, the warnings, and the work. Jude's readers, then and now, are invited to live in the space those two statements define, in confidence that the one who kept them from the beginning will keep them to the end.

To Him be glory. Amen.


# Part Two: Reference {.unnumbered .part}


# Appendix A. Significant Textual Variants in Jude {.unnumbered}

This appendix collects the variation units in Jude that affect translation or interpretation. For each unit it gives the reading of the SBL Greek New Testament (SBLGNT), the Nestle–Aland 28th edition (NA28), the Robinson–Pierpont Byzantine text (RP), and the Textus Receptus (TR), and it indicates which reading the LSV follows. The data come from the SBLGNT apparatus, the updated apparatus citing NA28 in the eBible.org SBLGNT edition, and the Robinson–Pierpont and TR texts themselves.[^aa-1] Approximate manuscript percentages, where given, come from the Text-Critical Greek New Testament notes. They count manuscripts without weighing them and are included only to show how a reading is distributed.

**How to read the tables.** "+" means "adds," and "–" means "omits." "Byz" refers to the Byzantine majority text.

## A.1 Summary table

| v. | SBLGNT | NA28 | RP (Byzantine) | TR | LSV follows | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ἠγαπημένοις "loved" | same | ἡγιασμένοις "sanctified" | ἡγιασμένοις | SBLGNT/NA28 ("loved") | Moderate: affects the letter's love theme |
| 3 | κοινῆς ἡμῶν σωτηρίας "our common salvation" | same | – ἡμῶν | – ἡμῶν | SBLGNT/NA28 | Minor |
| 4 | δεσπότην "Master" | same | + θεόν "God" | + θεόν | SBLGNT/NA28 | Moderate: KJV reads two persons |
| 5 | εἰδότας ὑμᾶς ἅπαξ πάντα ὅτι Ἰησοῦς | same (NA27: … ὅτι ὁ κύριος ἅπαξ …) | ἅπαξ τοῦτο ὅτι ὁ κύριος | same as RP | SBLGNT/NA28 ("Jesus") | **Major**: Christology |
| 7 | τρόπον τούτοις | same | τούτοις τρόπον | same as RP | — | Word order only |
| 12 | οἱ ἐν ταῖς ἀγάπαις | same | – οἱ | – οἱ | — | Minor (grammar of σπιλάδες) |
| 12 | παραφερόμεναι "carried away" | same | same | περιφερόμεναι "carried about" | — | Minor |
| 13 | εἰς αἰῶνα | same | same | εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα | — | None in meaning |
| 15 | πάντας τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς "all the impious" | πᾶσαν ψυχήν "every soul" | + αὐτῶν "their" | + αὐτῶν; ἐξελέγξαι | RP ("all their impious ones") | Moderate: relation to 1 Enoch 1:9 |
| 16 | αὐτῶν | ἑαυτῶν | αὐτῶν | αὐτῶν | — | None in meaning |
| 18 | ἐπ' ἐσχάτου χρόνου | same (NA27 adds τοῦ) | ἐν ἐσχάτῳ χρόνῳ; + ὅτι | same as RP | — | None in meaning |
| 19 | οἱ ἀποδιορίζοντες | same | same | + ἑαυτούς "themselves" | SBLGNT/RP | Moderate: "separate themselves" (KJV) vs. "cause divisions" |
| 20 | word order | same | different order | same as RP | — | None in meaning |
| 22–23 | three clauses, ἐλεᾶτε … σῴζετε … ἐλεᾶτε | same | two clauses, ἐλεεῖτε διακρινόμενοι … ἐν φόβῳ σῴζετε | same as RP | SBLGNT/NA28 (three clauses) | **Major**: number of groups; mercy vs. rebuke |
| 24 | ὑμᾶς "you" | same | αὐτούς "them" | ὑμᾶς | SBLGNT/TR ("you") | Minor |
| 25 | μόνῳ θεῷ "only God" | same | + σοφῷ "wise" | + σοφῷ | RP/TR ("only wise God") | Minor: assimilation to Rom 16:27 |
| 25 | διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ τοῦ κυρίου ἡμῶν | same | – | – | RP/TR (omits) | Moderate: glory "through Jesus Christ" |
| 25 | πρὸ παντὸς τοῦ αἰῶνος "before all time" | same | – | – | RP/TR (omits) | Moderate: glory before time |

## A.2 Notes on the major units

**Jude 1: "loved" or "sanctified."** About 90% of manuscripts read "sanctified." The critical editions all read "loved," judging it the earlier and harder reading. "Sanctified in God the Father" looks like a scribal improvement of the unusual "loved in God the Father," perhaps influenced by 1 Cor 1:2. See Chapter 1.

**Jude 4: "only Master [God] and Lord."** The added θεόν appears in the Byzantine tradition and produced the KJV's "denying the only Lord God, and our Lord Jesus Christ." Since a single article governs the nouns, even the longer text most naturally refers all the titles to Jesus, as the WEB's rendering of the Majority Text shows: "our only Master, God, and Lord, Jesus Christ." See Chapter 2.

**Jude 5: "Jesus" or "the Lord."** This is the most discussed variant in the letter. "Jesus" is read by important early manuscripts, including Codex Alexandrinus and Codex Vaticanus. The early papyrus P72 has "God Christ." Most later manuscripts have "the Lord." The editors of NA28 adopted "Jesus," reversing NA27, and the SBLGNT reads it too. The placement of "once" also varies. See the deep dive in Chapter 3.

**Jude 15: "all the impious" or "every soul."** NA28's "every soul" corresponds to 1 Enoch 1:9's "all flesh" (Charles). SBLGNT's "all the impious" matches Jude's emphasis on "impious." The Byzantine text adds "their." See Chapter 8.

**Jude 19: "who cause divisions" or "who separate themselves."** The TR's added ἑαυτούς creates the KJV's "These be they who separate themselves," suggesting withdrawal. The critical and Byzantine texts lack it, which gives "these are the ones who cause divisions." See Chapter 10.

**Jude 22–23: two groups or three?** Five main forms survive. The SBLGNT, NA28, and LSV have three clauses (mercy, rescue, mercy with fear). Westcott–Hort, the Byzantine text, and P72 have two clauses in different arrangements. Tregelles and the Tyndale House Greek New Testament (as reported) read "refute" (ἐλέγχετε) instead of "show mercy" in the first clause. Clement of Alexandria's paraphrase suggests a two-part text. See the deep dive in Chapter 12.

**Jude 25: the shape of the doxology.** The critical text's "through Jesus Christ our Lord" and "before all time" are absent from the Byzantine text and the LSV. The Byzantine "wise" is probably borrowed from Rom 16:27. See Chapter 13.

## A.3 What the variants show about the LSV

The LSV states in its preface that its New Testament is based on the Textus Receptus and the Majority Text, departing from them "in certain, specific instances … where the evidence seems incontrovertible."[^aa-2] Jude illustrates this policy well. In vv. 1, 3, 4, 5, and 22–23 the LSV adopts readings of the critical editions against the Byzantine majority. In vv. 15 and 25 it keeps the Byzantine readings. Readers of this book should therefore keep in mind that the LSV's English does not always correspond to a single printed Greek edition. The commentary notes every place where that matters.

## A.4 How significant are these variants?

None of Jude's variants affects any central Christian doctrine. Several affect how particular verses are read: whether Jesus is named as Israel's deliverer (v. 5), how many groups the readers are to help (vv. 22–23), and whether glory is ascribed to God "through Jesus Christ" and ‘before all time’ (v. 25). Honest reading includes knowing where the text is uncertain, and good textual scholarship lets readers see how much of the text is secure. For Jude, by any measure, almost all of it is.

[^aa-1]: SBLGNT apparatus, <https://www.sblgnt.com/download/86-Jud-APP.txt>; eBible.org SBLGNT edition (files dated 30 October 2026) with NA28 notes; Robinson–Pierpont 2018 and the Textus Receptus (eBible.org); Text-Critical Greek NT notes (eBible.org `grctcgnt`). Witnesses such as P72, Alexandrinus, and Vaticanus are named from the standard critical literature; see the verification notes in the Bibliography.

[^aa-2]: LSV preface, <https://www.lsvbible.com/p/the-preface-to-literal-standard-version.html> (accessed 7 November 2026).

This appendix summarizes the main interpretive questions discussed in the commentary. For each one it lists the principal options and the position this book takes, with the chapter where the evidence is discussed. "Preferred" means the reading this book judges most probable. It does not mean the other options lack merit.

| 3 | What is "the faith"? | the content of the gospel and its moral shape; personal trust | content (with its moral shape) | 2 |
| 4 | "Written beforehand" | (a) in earlier prophecies; (b) in heavenly books; (c) predestined individually | (a) | 2 |
| 4 | "Only Master and Lord" | both titles of Jesus; or (TR) God and Jesus | both of Jesus | 2 |
| 5 | Who saved the people from Egypt? | Jesus; the Lord; God | Jesus (textual and theological discussion) | 3 |

| 7 | Antecedent of "these" | the angels; the intruders | the angels | 3 |

| 8 | "Lordship" | the Lord's authority; angelic powers; church leaders | the Lord's authority | 4 |

| 9 | "A judgment of slander" | (a) a slanderous judgment; (b) a condemnation for slander | both presented; same lesson | 4 |

| 19 | ἀποδιορίζοντες | cause divisions; make distinctions; separate themselves (TR) | cause divisions / make distinctions | 10 |
| 19 | ψυχικοί | the opponents' own term turned back; Jude's own term | possibly ironic; uncertain | 10 |
| 20 | "Praying in the Holy Spirit" | Spirit-led prayer generally; charismatic prayer; prayer in the sphere of the Spirit | Spirit-led prayer (others compatible) | 11 |
| 21 | "The love of God" | God's love for us; our love for God | God's love (primary) | 11 |
| 22 | διακρινομένους | doubting/wavering; disputing | wavering | 12 |
| 22–23 | Two groups or three? | three-clause text; two-clause forms | three (with uncertainty) | 12 |
| 25 | Supplied verb | "is" (declaration); "be" (wish) | both senses present | 13 |


# Appendix C. Jude and 2 Peter: The Parallel Passages {.unnumbered}

This appendix sets the parallel passages side by side in the Literal Standard Version. The texts are generated directly from the LSV source files by `tools/make_parallels.py`, so they are exact. For discussion of the relationship between the two letters, see the Historical and Literary Introduction, §8.

**Summary of the evidence.** The two letters share a sequence of themes, images, and rare words. Three Greek words occur in the New Testament only in these two letters: συνευωχέομαι ("feast together"), ὑπέρογκος ("swollen"), and ἐμπαίκτης ("scoffer").[^ac-1] Jude contains material not found in 2 Peter: the quotation of Enoch (vv. 14–15), the named dispute of Michael and the Devil over Moses' body (v. 9), Cain and Korah (v. 11), the wild waves and wandering stars (v. 13), and the closing exhortations to mercy (vv. 20–23). 2 Peter adds the flood and Noah (2:5), Lot (2:7–8), Balaam's donkey (2:16), and the scoffers' question about Christ's coming (3:4).

## Jude 2 ∥ 2 Pet 1:2

> **2** mercy and peace and love be multiplied to you!
>
> — Jude 2, LSV

> **1:2** Grace and peace be multiplied to you in the acknowledgment of God and of Jesus our Lord!
>
> — 2 Pet 1:2, LSV

*Observation:* Same wish formula ("be multiplied"); Jude's triad differs.

## Jude 4 ∥ 2 Pet 2:1–3

> **4** for there came in certain men stealthily, having been written beforehand to this judgment long ago, impious, perverting the grace of our God to licentiousness, and denying our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ.
>
> — Jude 4, LSV

> **2:1** But there also came false prophets among the people, as there will also be false teachers among you, who will stealthily bring in destructive sects, even denying the Master who bought them, bringing quick destruction to themselves, **2:2** and many will follow out their destructive ways, because of whom the way of the truth will be spoken of [as] evil, **2:3** and in covetousness, with forged words, they will make merchandise of you, whose judgment of old is not idle, and their destruction does not slumber.
>
> — 2 Pet 2:1–3, LSV

*Observation:* Intruders "came in … stealthily" / "will stealthily bring in"; both deny the Master (δεσπότης). Jude: past tense; 2 Peter: future.

## Jude 5 ∥ 2 Pet 1:12

> **5** Now I intend to remind you, you once having known all this, that Jesus, having saved a people out of the land of Egypt, He secondly destroyed those not having believed;
>
> — Jude 5, LSV

> **1:12** Therefore, I will always be ready to remind you about these things, although you have known [them] and have been established in the present truth,
>
> — 2 Pet 1:12, LSV

*Observation:* Both remind readers of what they already know.

## Jude 6 ∥ 2 Pet 2:4

> **6** messengers also, those who did not keep their own principality, but left their proper dwelling, He has kept in eternal bonds under darkness until [the] judgment of [the] great day,
>
> — Jude 6, LSV

> **2:4** For if God did not spare messengers having sinned, but having cast [them] down to Tartarus with chains of deepest gloom, delivered [them], having been reserved to judgment,
>
> — 2 Pet 2:4, LSV

*Observation:* Sinning angels kept in gloom for judgment. 2 Peter adds "Tartarus."

## Jude 7 ∥ 2 Pet 2:6–8

> **7** as Sodom and Gomorrah, and the cities around them, in like manner to these [messengers], having given themselves to whoredom, and having gone after other flesh, have been set before [as] an example, undergoing the justice of continuous fire.
>
> — Jude 7, LSV

> **2:6** and having turned the cities of Sodom and Gomorrah to ashes, condemned with an overthrow, having set [them as] an example to those about to be impious, **2:7** and He rescued righteous Lot, worn down by the conduct of the lawless in licentiousness, **2:8** for the righteous [man] dwelling among them was tormented in [his] righteous soul, day by day, in seeing and hearing unlawful works—
>
> — 2 Pet 2:6–8, LSV

*Observation:* Sodom and Gomorrah as example. 2 Peter adds the rescue of Lot and inserts the flood (2:5) to make a biblical sequence.

## Jude 8 ∥ 2 Pet 2:10

> **8** In like manner, nevertheless, those dreaming indeed defile the flesh, and they disregard lordship, and they speak evil of glorious [ones],
>
> — Jude 8, LSV

> **2:10** and chiefly those following after the flesh in lust [and] defilement, and despising lordship. Bold, self-pleased, they are not afraid to speak evil of glorious ones,
>
> — 2 Pet 2:10, LSV

*Observation:* Defiling the flesh, despising lordship, slandering "glorious ones."

## Jude 9 ∥ 2 Pet 2:11

> **9** yet Michael, the chief messenger, when disputing with the Devil, reasoning about the body of Moses, did not dare to bring against [him] a judgment of slander, but said, “The LORD rebuke you!”
>
> — Jude 9, LSV

> **2:11** whereas messengers, being greater in strength and power, do not bear a slanderous judgment against them before the LORD;
>
> — 2 Pet 2:11, LSV

*Observation:* Angels do not bring a slanderous judgment. 2 Peter omits Michael, the Devil, and Moses.

## Jude 10 ∥ 2 Pet 2:12

> **10** But these, indeed, whatever [things] they have not known, they speak evil of; and whatever [they know], like the irrational beasts, they understand naturally—in these they are corrupted;
>
> — Jude 10, LSV

> **2:12** and these, as irrational natural beasts, made to be caught and destroyed—in what things they are ignorant of, slandering—in their destruction will be destroyed;
>
> — 2 Pet 2:12, LSV

*Observation:* Slandering what they do not know; irrational beasts; destruction.

## Jude 11 ∥ 2 Pet 2:15–16

> **11** woe to them! Because they went on in the way of Cain, and they rushed to the deceit of Balaam for reward, and in the controversy of Korah they perished.
>
> — Jude 11, LSV

> **2:15** having forsaken a right way, they went astray, having followed in the way of Balaam the [son] of Bosor, who loved a reward of unrighteousness, **2:16** and had a rebuke of his own iniquity—a mute donkey, having spoken in man’s voice, forbid the madness of the prophet.
>
> — 2 Pet 2:15–16, LSV

*Observation:* Balaam and his reward. 2 Peter omits Cain and Korah and adds the donkey.

## Jude 12 ∥ 2 Pet 2:13; 2 Pet 2:17

> **12** These are stains at your love-feasts, feasting together with you without fear, shepherding themselves; waterless clouds, being carried away by winds; autumnal trees without fruit, having died twice, having been uprooted;
>
> — Jude 12, LSV

> **2:13** doing unjustly, [they will receive] a reward of unrighteousness, esteeming pleasure in the day, [and] luxury—[they are] spots and blemishes, reveling in their deceits, feasting with you, … **2:17** These are wells without water, and clouds driven by a storm, to whom the deepest gloom of darkness has been kept throughout the age;
>
> — 2 Pet 2:13; 2 Pet 2:17, LSV

*Observation:* "Feasting together" (only here in the NT); "stains/reefs" vs. "spots and blemishes"; "love-feasts" vs. "deceits" (a one-letter difference in Greek); waterless clouds vs. "wells without water, and clouds driven by a storm."

## Jude 13 ∥ 2 Pet 2:17

> **13** wild waves of a sea, foaming up their own shameful [deeds]; stars going astray, for whom the gloom of the darkness throughout the age has been kept.
>
> — Jude 13, LSV

> **2:17** These are wells without water, and clouds driven by a storm, to whom the deepest gloom of darkness has been kept throughout the age;
>
> — 2 Pet 2:17, LSV

*Observation:* "The gloom of the darkness … has been kept" applied in 2 Peter to the wells and mists.

## Jude 16 ∥ 2 Pet 2:18

> **16** These are discontented grumblers, walking according to their own lusts; and their mouth speaks swollen words, giving admiration to persons for the sake of profit;
>
> — Jude 16, LSV

> **2:18** for speaking swollen words of vanity, they entice in desires of the flesh—licentiousness, those who had truly escaped from those conducting themselves in error,
>
> — 2 Pet 2:18, LSV

*Observation:* "Swollen words" (ὑπέρογκα; only here in the NT).

## Jude 17–18 ∥ 2 Pet 3:1–3

> **17** but you, beloved, remember the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ, **18** that they said to you, that in the last time there will be scoffers, following after their own lusts of impieties,
>
> — Jude 17–18, LSV

> **3:1** This [is] now, beloved, a second letter I write to you, in both which I stir up your pure mind in reminding [you] **3:2** to be mindful of the sayings said before by the holy prophets, and of the command of us the apostles of the LORD and Savior, **3:3** knowing this first, that there will come scoffers in the last days, going on according to their own desires,
>
> — 2 Pet 3:1–3, LSV

*Observation:* Remembering the apostles' words; scoffers in the last time/days following their own desires (ἐμπαῖκται; only here in the NT). 2 Peter adds the prophets and specifies mockery of the promise of Christ's coming.

## Jude 24–25 ∥ 2 Pet 3:17–18

> **24** And to Him who is able to guard you without stumbling, and to set [you] in the presence of His glory unblemished, in gladness, **25** to the only wise God our Savior, [is] glory and greatness, power and authority, both now and forever! Amen.
>
> — Jude 24–25, LSV

> **3:17** You, then, beloved, knowing before, take heed, lest, together with the error of the impious being led away, you may fall from your own steadfastness, **3:18** and increase in grace, and in the knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ; to Him [is] the glory both now, and to the day of the age! Amen.
>
> — 2 Pet 3:17–18, LSV

*Observation:* Both close with a warning against falling and a doxology. 2 Peter's doxology is addressed to Christ.

## Material in Jude without a parallel in 2 Peter

- The greeting's description of believers as called, loved, and kept (v. 1).
- The exodus example, with the reading "Jesus" (v. 5).
- The story of Michael and the Devil over Moses' body (v. 9).
- Cain and Korah (v. 11).
- "Wild waves of a sea" and "stars going astray" (v. 13).
- The quotation from 1 Enoch (vv. 14–15).
- "Grumblers" and flattery (v. 16).
- "Causing divisions … not having the Spirit" (v. 19).
- Building, praying in the Holy Spirit, keeping in God's love (vv. 20–21).
- Mercy for the doubting and rescue from the fire (vv. 22–23).

# Glossary of Greek and Theological Terms {.unnumbered}

Greek words are listed by transliteration, with the Greek form and the verse in Jude where it appears. Lexical summaries draw on Abbott-Smith's *Manual Greek Lexicon of the New Testament* (1922) unless noted.

## Greek words

**agapē** (ἀγάπη), vv. 2, 12, 21. "Love." In the plural (v. 12), "love feasts," the community's shared meals. See Chapter 6.

**agapētoi** (ἀγαπητοί), vv. 3, 17, 20. "Beloved," Jude's address to his readers.

**agalliasis** (ἀγαλλίασις), v. 24. "Exultation, exuberant joy."

**akarpos** (ἄκαρπος), v. 12. "Fruitless."

**alogos** (ἄλογος), v. 10. "Without reason"; τὰ ἄλογα ζῷα, "irrational animals."

**amōmos** (ἄμωμος), v. 24. "Without blemish," used of sacrificial animals, and morally "faultless."

**antilogia** (ἀντιλογία), v. 11. "Contradiction, dispute, rebellion." In the LXX it is used for the "water of contention" (Meribah).

**anydros** (ἄνυδρος), v. 12. "Waterless."

**aphobōs** (ἀφόβως), v. 12. "Without fear," here meaning without reverence.

**apodiorizō** (ἀποδιορίζω), v. 19. "To mark off; to make divisions." NT hapax.

**aptaistos** (ἄπταιστος), v. 24. "Without stumbling, sure-footed." NT hapax.

**archangelos** (ἀρχάγγελος), v. 9. "Archangel, chief messenger" (LSV). Used of Michael.

**archē** (ἀρχή), v. 6. "Beginning; rule, domain." The angels' assigned position (LSV "principality").

**asebēs, asebeia, asebeō** (ἀσεβής, ἀσέβεια, ἀσεβέω), vv. 4, 15, 18. "Impious/ungodly," "impiety," "to act impiously." Jude's key description of the intruders.

**aselgeia** (ἀσέλγεια), v. 4. "Licentiousness, shameless excess."

**atheteō** (ἀθετέω), v. 8. "To set aside, reject."

**aidios** (ἀΐδιος), v. 6. "Everlasting."

**aiōn, aiōnios** (αἰών, αἰώνιος), vv. 7, 13, 21, 25. "Age" and "age-long, eternal." The LSV renders αἰώνιος "continuous."

**blasphēmeō, blasphēmia** (βλασφημέω, βλασφημία), vv. 8–10. "To slander, revile"; "slander." Used of abusive speech against anyone, not only against God.

**deigma** (δεῖγμα), v. 7. "A specimen, example (as a warning)." NT hapax.

**despotēs** (δεσπότης), v. 4. "Master, owner, sovereign." Used of Jesus.

**diabolos** (διάβολος), v. 9. "Slanderer, accuser; the Devil."

**diakrinomai** (διακρίνομαι), vv. 9, 22. ‘To dispute’ (v. 9) or ‘to doubt, waver’ (v. 22).

**dikē** (δίκη), v. 7. "Justice; the penalty carried out."

**doulos** (δοῦλος), v. 1. "Slave, servant." Also an honorific ("servant of the LORD").

**doxa, doxai** (δόξα, δόξαι), vv. 8, 24, 25. "Glory"; in the plural (v. 8), "glorious ones," that is, angels.

**ekporneuō** (ἐκπορνεύω), v. 7. "To give oneself up to sexual immorality." NT hapax.

**ekrizoō** (ἐκριζόω), v. 12. "To uproot."

**eleos, eleaō** (ἔλεος, ἐλεάω), vv. 2, 21, 22, 23. "Mercy"; "to show mercy." In the LXX ἔλεος chiefly translates Hebrew *ḥesed*.

**elenchō** (ἐλέγχω), v. 15. "To expose, convict."

**empaiktēs** (ἐμπαίκτης), v. 18. "Scoffer, mocker." In the NT only here and 2 Pet 3:3.

**enypniazomai** (ἐνυπνιάζομαι), v. 8. "To dream." In the LXX, used of the false prophet as "dreamer" (Deut 13).

**epagōnizomai** (ἐπαγωνίζομαι), v. 3. "To contend for." NT hapax.

**epaphrizō** (ἐπαφρίζω), v. 13. "To foam up." NT hapax.

**epithymia** (ἐπιθυμία), vv. 16, 18. "Desire, craving."

**epoikodomeō** (ἐποικοδομέω), v. 20. "To build upon."

**gongystēs** (γογγυστής), v. 16. "Grumbler, murmurer." NT hapax.

**hagioi** (ἅγιοι), vv. 3, 14. "Holy ones": God's people (v. 3) or angels (v. 14).

**hagiōtatos** (ἁγιώτατος), v. 20. "Most holy." The only superlative of ἅγιος in the NT.

**hapax** (ἅπαξ), vv. 3, 5. "Once, once for all."

**harpazō** (ἁρπάζω), v. 23. "To seize, snatch."

**hyperonkos** (ὑπέρογκος), v. 16. "Swollen, excessive; arrogant (of speech)."

**hypechō** (ὑπέχω), v. 7. "To undergo, suffer (a penalty)." NT hapax.

**katenōpion** (κατενώπιον), v. 24. "Before, in the presence of."

**klētos** (κλητός), v. 1. "Called."

**koinos** (κοινός), v. 3. "Common, shared."

**kratos** (κράτος), v. 25. "Power, dominion." Common in doxologies.

**kyriotēs** (κυριότης), v. 8. "Lordship, dominion."

**megalōsynē** (μεγαλωσύνη), v. 25. "Greatness, majesty."

**mempsimoiros** (μεμψίμοιρος), v. 16. "Complaining of one's lot, querulous." NT hapax.

**metatithēmi** (μετατίθημι), v. 4. "To change, transfer; to turn one thing into a pretext for another."

**miainō** (μιαίνω), v. 8. "To defile."

**oikētērion** (οἰκητήριον), v. 6. "Dwelling, habitation."

**ouai** (οὐαί), v. 11. "Woe!" The prophetic announcement of judgment.

**paradidōmi** (παραδίδωμι), v. 3. "To hand over, deliver." The technical term for passing on tradition.

**parapherō** (παραφέρω), v. 12. "To carry away."

**pareisdyō** (παρεισδύω), v. 4. "To slip in secretly." NT hapax.

**phthinopōrinos** (φθινοπωρινός), v. 12. "Of late autumn." NT hapax.

**phylassō** (φυλάσσω), v. 24. "To guard, protect." Near synonym of τηρέω.

**physikōs** (φυσικῶς), v. 10. "Naturally, by instinct." NT hapax.

**planē** (πλάνη), v. 11. "Error, deception, wandering."

**planētēs** (πλανήτης), v. 13. "Wanderer"; ἀστέρες πλανῆται, "wandering stars." The source of English "planet." NT hapax.

**plēthynō** (πληθύνω), v. 2. "To multiply." πληθυνθείη: "may it be multiplied" (optative).

**poimainō** (ποιμαίνω), v. 12. "To shepherd, tend."

**prographō** (προγράφω), v. 4. "To write beforehand."

**psychikos** (ψυχικός), v. 19. "Natural, soulish, unspiritual."

**sklēros** (σκληρός), v. 15. "Hard, harsh"; "harsh words."

**sōtēr** (σωτήρ), v. 25. "Savior." A title also given to kings and emperors in the Greco-Roman world.

**spilas** (σπιλάς), v. 12. "Reef, rock in the sea"; in later Greek, "stain." NT hapax. See Chapter 6.

**spiloō** (σπιλόω), v. 23. "To stain, defile."

**syneuōcheomai** (συνευωχέομαι), v. 12. "To feast together." In the NT only here and 2 Pet 2:13.

**tēreō** (τηρέω), vv. 1, 6, 13, 21. "To keep, guard, preserve." Jude's key word.

**zophos** (ζόφος), vv. 6, 13. "Deep gloom," associated with the underworld.

## Grammatical and theological terms

**Aorist.** A Greek verb form that presents an action as a whole, without reference to its duration.

**Canon.** The list of books a community recognizes as Scripture.

**Critical text.** A Greek text reconstructed by editors from the manuscripts in order to approximate the earliest recoverable text (for example, NA28, SBLGNT).

**Byzantine text / Majority Text.** The form of the Greek text found in the majority of (mostly later) manuscripts, edited by Robinson and Pierpont among others.

**Doxology.** A formula ascribing glory to God.

**Etymological fallacy.** The mistake of assuming that a word's origin or component parts determine its meaning in use.

**Hapax legomenon** (plural *hapax legomena*). A word that occurs only once in a given body of literature, here the New Testament.

**LXX (Septuagint).** The Greek translation of the Hebrew scriptures, used widely by Greek-speaking Jews and early Christians.

**Optative.** A Greek verb mood used for wishes and prayers. It is rare in the New Testament.

**Participle.** A verbal adjective ("having been loved," "praying").

**Perfect tense.** A Greek tense presenting a completed action with continuing results.

**Pre-existence (of Christ).** The belief that the Son existed before his human birth.

**Prophetic aorist.** The use of a past tense to describe a future event as certain.

**Pseudepigrapha.** Ancient Jewish (and Christian) writings attributed to biblical figures, such as 1 Enoch and the *Testament of Moses*.

**Pseudonymity.** Writing under another person's name.

**Second Temple Judaism.** Judaism from the rebuilding of the temple (late sixth century BC) to its destruction in AD 70.

**Targum.** An Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Hebrew scriptures.

**Textus Receptus (TR).** The printed Greek text that underlies the King James Version.

**Theophany.** An appearance of God.

**Type / typology.** A person or event in Scripture understood as a pattern of later persons or events.

**Watchers.** In 1 Enoch, the angels who descended to earth and took human wives (Gen 6:1–4).

# Bibliography {.unnumbered}

The bibliography is divided according to how each work was used in preparing this book. **Section 1** lists the texts and editions that were retrieved and read directly; specific passages relied on were checked against them. **Section 2** lists works known only through reliable secondary reports. **Section 3** lists standard scholarly works cited for widely known positions that could not be re-consulted during the research for this edition. Claims attributed to Section 3 works should be checked against the printed volumes before publication, and page numbers have deliberately been omitted for them. Full details of the verification process are in the project file `research/source-ledger.md`.

## 1. Texts and editions consulted directly

### Biblical texts

*The Holy Bible: Literal Standard Version*. Covenant Press and the Covenant Christian Coalition, 2020. eBible.org USFM edition, source files dated 30 October 2026. <https://ebible.org/englsv/>. Licensed CC BY-SA 4.0. Publisher site: <https://www.lsvbible.com/>.

Holmes, Michael W., ed. *The Greek New Testament: SBL Edition*. Atlanta: Society of Biblical Literature; Bellingham, WA: Logos Bible Software, 2010. Data version 1.2 (2023). <https://github.com/Faithlife/SBLGNT>. Licensed CC BY 4.0. Apparatus: <https://www.sblgnt.com/download/86-Jud-APP.txt>; updated apparatus with NA28 data in the eBible.org SBLGNT edition (`grcsbl`).

Tauber, J. K., ed. *MorphGNT: SBLGNT Edition*. Version 6.12. 2017. <https://github.com/morphgnt/sblgnt>. DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.376200.

Robinson, Maurice A., and William G. Pierpont. *The New Testament in the Original Greek: Byzantine Textform 2018*. Public domain. eBible.org edition (`grcmt`).

Robinson, Maurice A., text; Robert Adam Boyd, notes. *Text-Critical Greek New Testament*. Public domain. eBible.org edition (`grctcgnt`).

*Greek Textus Receptus*, with manuscript comparison notes by Adam Boyd. Public domain. eBible.org edition (`grctr`).

*World English Bible*. Public domain. eBible.org edition (`engwebp`).

*American Standard Version*. 1901. Public domain. eBible.org edition.

*Berean Standard Bible*. BSB Publishing. Public domain. eBible.org edition.

*King James Version* (1769 text). Public domain outside the United Kingdom. eBible.org edition.

Brenton, Lancelot C. L., ed. *The Septuagint with Apocrypha: Greek and English*. London: Bagster, 1851. Public domain. eBible.org editions (`grcbrent`, `eng-Brenton`).

### Jewish texts

Charles, R. H., trans. *The Book of Enoch*. London: SPCK, 1917. Wikisource transcription: <https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Book_of_Enoch_(Charles)>.

Etheridge, J. W., trans. *The Targums of Onkelos and Jonathan ben Uzziel on the Pentateuch*. London: Longman, Green, 1862–65. Genesis 1–6: <https://www.targum.info/pj/pjgen1-6.htm>.

Josephus. *The Antiquities of the Jews*. Translated by William Whiston. Project Gutenberg #2848. <https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/2848>.

Philo of Alexandria. *On Abraham*. Translated by C. D. Yonge. <https://www.earlyjewishwritings.com/text/philo/book22.html>.

*The Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs*. Translated by R. Sinker. In *Ante-Nicene Fathers*, vol. 8. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0801.htm>.

### Early Christian, Greek, and Latin texts

Athanasius. *Festal Letter* 39. NPNF², vol. 4. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806039.htm>.

Augustine. *The City of God*, book 15. NPNF¹, vol. 2. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/120115.htm>.

Clement of Alexandria. *Comments on the Epistle of Jude* (*Adumbrationes*). ANF, vol. 2, "Fragments." <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0211.htm>.

———. *The Instructor* (*Paedagogus*), book 2. ANF, vol. 2. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/02092.htm>.

*The Didache*. Translated by M. B. Riddle. ANF, vol. 7. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0714.htm>.

Eusebius. *Ecclesiastical History*, books 2, 3, 6. NPNF², vol. 1. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250102.htm>, <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250103.htm>, <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250106.htm>.

Ignatius of Antioch. *To the Smyrnaeans*. ANF, vol. 1. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0109.htm>.

Jerome. *On Illustrious Men* (*De viris illustribus*). NPNF², vol. 3. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2708.htm>.

Muratorian Fragment. In "Fragments of Caius," ANF, vol. 5. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0510.htm>.

Origen. *Commentary on Matthew*, book 10. ANF, vol. 9. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/101610.htm>.

———. *On First Principles* (*De principiis*), book 3. ANF, vol. 4. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/04123.htm>.

Pliny the Younger. *Epistulae* 2.6 and 10.96. Latin text: The Latin Library, <https://www.thelatinlibrary.com/pliny.ep2.html>, <https://www.thelatinlibrary.com/pliny.ep10.html>.

Tertullian. *Apology*. ANF, vol. 3. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0301.htm>.

———. *On the Apparel of Women* (*De cultu feminarum*). ANF, vol. 4. <https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0402.htm>.

Theophrastus. *The Characters of Theophrastus*. Translated by Charles E. Bennett and William A. Hammond. New York: Longmans, Green, 1902. Project Gutenberg #58242.

### Reformation commentary

Calvin, John. *Commentaries on the Catholic Epistles*. Translated by John Owen. Edinburgh: Calvin Translation Society, 1855. CCEL: <https://www.ccel.org/ccel/calvin/calcom45.html>.

Luther, Martin. "Preface to the Epistles of St. James and St. Jude" (1522). English translation as reproduced at <https://www.bible-researcher.com/antilegomena.html> (from *Luther's Works*, vol. 35; page not verified).

### Lexicon

Abbott-Smith, G. *A Manual Greek Lexicon of the New Testament*. Edinburgh: T&T Clark, 1922. Public domain. TEI edition: <https://github.com/translatable-exegetical-tools/Abbott-Smith>.

## 2. Works known through reliable secondary reports

"Book of Enoch." *Wikipedia*. Accessed 7 November 2026. Used for the summary of the manuscripts and canonical status of 1 Enoch.

"Testament of Moses." *Wikipedia*. Accessed 7 November 2026. Used for the summary of the Ceriani manuscript and of R. H. Charles's analysis.

Mayor, Joseph B. *The Epistle of St. Jude and the Second Epistle of St. Peter*. London: Macmillan, 1907. Cited through Abbott-Smith's references.

Moulton, J. H., and G. Milligan. *The Vocabulary of the Greek Testament*. London: Hodder & Stoughton, 1930. Cited through Abbott-Smith.

Flink, Timo. "Reconsidering the Text of Jude 5, 13, 15 and 18." *Filologia Neotestamentaria* 20 (2007): 95–125. Existence, topic, and argument reported in search results.

Milik, J. T. *The Books of Enoch: Aramaic Fragments of Qumrân Cave 4*. Oxford: Clarendon, 1976. Milik's judgment on Jude 14–15 is reported from secondary discussions.
